# Milo language specification

Generated from the test suite by `scripts/gen-spec.ts` — 1131 normative requirements (409 rejection, 722 behavioural). Do not edit this file; edit the program it cites, or that program's leading comment, and regenerate.

## Status of this document

This is a **specification by example**, and the distinction matters before anyone relies on it.

Each requirement names one concrete program and states what a conforming implementation must do
with it: reject it with a particular diagnostic, or accept it and produce particular output. Every
requirement is discharged by a test that runs on every commit, so this document cannot describe a
language the compiler does not implement — that is the property it is built to have, and the reason
it is generated rather than written.

What it does **not** provide:

- **A semantics.** There is no denotational or operational model here. These requirements constrain
  behaviour on the programs they name and say nothing about the infinitely many they do not.
- **Completeness.** A rule with no fixture states no requirement. The suite is the coverage, and
  `docs/language-reference.md` describes constructs this document may not yet pin.
- **Grammar.** The syntax a conforming implementation accepts is [`grammar.ebnf`](grammar.ebnf),
  which is separately gated against the compiler's own keyword set.

Requirement identifiers are keyed to the **file name** of the program, not to its diagnostic text,
so improving a message does not renumber the specification. Renaming or deleting a program is a
change to this document and shows up as one.

The key words **shall** and **conforming implementation** are used in their normative sense.

## Traceability

| requirement class | count | prefix | verified by |
|---|---|---|---|
| rejection | 409 | `MILO-E-` | `tests/run.test.ts` — *errors (type checker rejects)* |
| behavioural | 722 | `MILO-B-` | `tests/run.test.ts` — *fixtures (compile + run)* |

Every identifier below cites the program that states it and the suite that discharges it. A
requirement with no verification cannot appear here: the generator reads the annotations the test
driver reads, so an unverifiable claim has nowhere to come from.

## Rejection requirements

Programs a conforming implementation shall refuse to compile. The pinned text is a **substring** of the diagnostic, not the whole of it: the wording and formatting of a message are not normative, only that the implementation rejects the program and says why in terms that contain this text.

### MILO-E-adoptHeapOutsideForeign

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `undefined function 'adoptHeap'`

**Rationale.** Minting ownership of an arbitrary address is not a general escape hatch: outside std/foreign.milo the name means nothing. `adopt` adds the null test and the Option wrapping in readable Milo, and it is `@unsafe`; `let b = adoptHeap(p)` here would hand back the same owned box with neither.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/adoptHeapOutsideForeign.milo`](../tests/errors/adoptHeapOutsideForeign.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-adoptNeedsUnsafe

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `calling 'adopt' requires an unsafe block`

**Rationale.** `@unsafe` puts the proof obligation on the caller, and here the obligation is the sharpest one in the module: that this pointer came from a Milo allocation of this type and has not been adopted before. Every operation in the body is individually checkable and `adopt(p)` on a stack address, a C string or a pointer already adopted is still a lie the compiler cannot see.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/adoptNeedsUnsafe.milo`](../tests/errors/adoptNeedsUnsafe.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-adoptVecOutsideForeign

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `undefined function 'adoptVec'`

**Rationale.** The slice half of the same seam. Both intrinsic names are gated by one file test, so this and adoptHeapOutsideForeign.milo fall together, which is the point of checking both: a gate written per name is a gate that loses a name.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/adoptVecOutsideForeign.milo`](../tests/errors/adoptVecOutsideForeign.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-adoptedUseAfterMove

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `use of moved variable 'box'`

**Rationale.** The adopted box is owned in the ordinary way, which means the ordinary rules apply to it. Nothing about arriving through a raw pointer makes it exempt: moving it out and then reading it is the same error the move checker gives for `Heap(value)`, and that equivalence is the whole claim `adopt` makes.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/adoptedUseAfterMove.milo`](../tests/errors/adoptedUseAfterMove.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-aliasMutContainerElement

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `borrowed mutably twice`

**Rationale.** Passing a Vec element and the Vec itself as two &mut args aliases the element into the container; a push inside reallocs and frees the element's storage — a use-after-free. The checker must reject the overlapping mutable borrows.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/aliasMutContainerElement.milo`](../tests/errors/aliasMutContainerElement.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-aliasTakesNoTypeArgs

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `'Meters' is not a generic type`

**Rationale.** The mirror of the arity error: an ordinary alias stands for one type, so arguments have nowhere to go. This message predates generic aliases and is already the right one, so there is no second rule for it - the fixture is here to keep it that way now that SOME aliases do take arguments.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/aliasTakesNoTypeArgs.milo`](../tests/errors/aliasTakesNoTypeArgs.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-arenaBrandMixup

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `'get' argument 1: expected HandleB, got HandleA`

**Rationale.** Two arenas of the same payload type hand out the same Handle<Node>, so a handle from graph A type-checks against graph B and only the runtime id check says no. Branding each arena with its own wrapper pair makes the mixup a compile error. The `_` fields keep the brand unstrippable from any other file (private fields).

*Program:* [`tests/errors/arenaBrandMixup.milo`](../tests/errors/arenaBrandMixup.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-arenaFreezeUseAfterMove

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `use of moved variable 'a'`

**Rationale.** freeze() consumes the arena. Reaching for the old binding afterwards is the compile-time half of the guarantee: no path survives through which a slot could be freed after the freeze, so a frozen handle cannot go stale.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/arenaFreezeUseAfterMove.milo`](../tests/errors/arenaFreezeUseAfterMove.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-arrayLiteralElementType

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `array element has type string, but the array is declared [i64; 2]`

**Rationale.** Both array-literal paths called `checkExprWithHint(elem, hint.element)` and threw the answer away, so nothing ever compared them. `var x: [i64; 2] = ["a", "b"]` type-checked, and the mismatch surfaced as an LLVM error — `'%t.3' defined with type '%String' but expected 'i64'` — which is a type error escaping to clang and reported in a language the user does not write. `Vec<i64> = ["a"]` had the identical shape. Found by tests/typeAnnotationFidelity.test.ts, which asks whether a value of the WRONG shape can satisfy an annotation.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/arrayLiteralElementType.milo`](../tests/errors/arrayLiteralElementType.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-arrayNonCopyMove

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `use of moved variable 'x'`

**Rationale.** The element rule is what makes "arrays are Copy" safe: `[u8; N]` is Copy, but an array of NON-Copy elements must still move, or the two `takes` calls below would each own the same heap strings and free them twice.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/arrayNonCopyMove.milo`](../tests/errors/arrayNonCopyMove.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-arrayOfRefs

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `an array of references is not expressible`

**Rationale.** Same dropped-modifier bug as nestedFixedArray, different modifier: the parser kept only the inner type's name, so `[&string; 2]` silently became `[string; 2]` — an OWNED array. References are second-class and cannot be stored, so an array of them has no representation at all, and accepting the annotation promised one.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/arrayOfRefs.milo`](../tests/errors/arrayOfRefs.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-brandMismatchNamesWrittenType

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `argument 1 of 'loadUser': expected Id<UserTag>, got Id<OrderTag>`

**Rationale.** A phantom brand exists so that this line is rejected, which makes this the error a reader meets exactly when they are doing the right thing. It used to be spelled `expected Id_UserTag, got Id_OrderTag`: the monomorphized instance name, which is an implementation detail nobody wrote (backlog Tier 1 #33). Diagnostics format a type back to the source spelling; the mangled name stays internal to lookups.

### MILO-E-cLayoutBadArgs

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `expected two string arguments`

**Rationale.** Bare idents parse (that's @derive's arg form) but say nothing about which C type or header is meant, so @cLayout must reject them rather than skip the check.

### MILO-E-cLayoutBadHeader

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `is not a C header path`

**Rationale.** The header string is pasted into a generated '#include <...>', so it's constrained to a charset that can't break out of the include and inject C into the guard TU.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cLayoutBadHeader.milo`](../tests/errors/cLayoutBadHeader.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-cLayoutMismatch

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `C header disagrees`

**Rationale.** A wrong field width is the silent-corruption case @cLayout exists to catch: tv_sec as i32 still leaves tv_nsec at offset 8 (i64 alignment pads it), so every offset matches and only the field's own size gives the drift away.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cLayoutMismatch.milo`](../tests/errors/cLayoutMismatch.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-cLayoutNoSuchField

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `a declaration does not match the C header it claims to describe`

**Rationale.** Naming a field C doesn't have makes offsetof itself ill-formed. Apple Clang and GCC phrase the underlying error differently, so assert Milo's stable wrapper.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cLayoutNoSuchField.milo`](../tests/errors/cLayoutNoSuchField.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

**Rationale.** The attribute set is closed. A silently-ignored one is the failure @cLayout exists to close — a typo'd @cOpaque would leave the field checked against a C struct that has no such member, which is a confusing error at best.

### MILO-E-cSigArityMismatch

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `takes 2 parameters, the Milo declaration takes 3`

**Rationale.** Arity is checkable with no header in the picture, and it has to be: the guard TU compares C parameter i against Milo parameter i, so a signature listing a different number of parameters would silently shift every width comparison by one and report a mismatch against the wrong parameter — or, worse, agree by luck.

### MILO-E-cSigParamPointee

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `Milo writes through a *u32 (4-byte pointee)`

**Rationale.** asserts on a diagnostic that quotes string.h's strlen spelling; the MSVC CRT declares it differently, so the compile fails for a different reason. An out-param's pointee width IS the contract — it is how many bytes the callee writes into the caller's frame. Nothing else in the pipeline can see this: the ABI passes one word whatever the pointee, so a wrong pointee links, runs, and scribbles.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cSigParamPointee.milo`](../tests/errors/cSigParamPointee.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-cSigParamWidth

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `Milo declares i64 (8 bytes)`

**Rationale.** asserts on a diagnostic that quotes stdlib.h's abs spelling. A scalar parameter declared too wide is an ABI mismatch the linker cannot see.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cSigParamWidth.milo`](../tests/errors/cSigParamWidth.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-cSigWrongC

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `is not what unistd.h declares`

**Rationale.** asserts on a diagnostic that quotes unistd.h; the MSVC CRT has no such header, so the compile fails for a different reason and proves nothing. The stated C signature is wrong: sysconf returns `long`, not `int`. Checked exactly against the header via __builtin_types_compatible_p.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cSigWrongC.milo`](../tests/errors/cSigWrongC.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-cSigWrongMilo

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `Milo declares a 4-byte return`

**Rationale.** asserts on a diagnostic that quotes unistd.h; the MSVC CRT has no such header, so the compile fails for a different reason and proves nothing. The C signature is right, but the Milo declaration disagrees with it: `long` is 8 bytes, i32 is 4. This is the Milo<->C mapping check — the half that a header-only comparison can't catch.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cSigWrongMilo.milo`](../tests/errors/cSigWrongMilo.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `must be an integer literal`

**Rationale.** Folding this in the compiler and asserting the result would compare Milo's arithmetic against itself — it proves nothing about what the header says. The point of @cValue is to check a hand-transcribed constant, so there has to be a transcription to check.

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `is not a constant`

**Rationale.** A `var` can be reassigned at runtime, so there is no fixed value for the guard to compare against the header — the assert would describe only the initializer.

### MILO-E-callSiteExclusivity

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `borrowed mutably and shared in the same call`

**Rationale.** Passing a variable as both a mutable borrow and the source of a shared borrow in one call is rejected: a mutation through &var could invalidate the & arg (e.g. push reallocates the Vec, leaving &v[0] dangling).

*Program:* [`tests/errors/callSiteExclusivity.milo`](../tests/errors/callSiteExclusivity.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-callbackParamType

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `callback parameter 1 is declared`

**Rationale.** Every Vec combinator built a `cbHint` describing what it would pass, handed it to `checkExprWithHint`, and at best asked whether the answer was a function at all. Nobody compared the PARAMETERS, so a closure could declare any type it liked and be handed something else. `v.each((x: &string) => print(x.len))` over a `Vec<i64>` was accepted and printed a pointer value; `v.map((s: &string) => s.len)` read the next element's bytes, and with a smaller allocation would read past the end. Both spellings the language does support stay legal: the hint's own type (`&i64`) and its by-value form (`i64`), which is how a Copy element is idiomatically taken.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/callbackParamType.milo`](../tests/errors/callbackParamType.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-cloneDeriveEnumClosurePayload

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `cannot derive Clone for 'E': variant 'F' payload 0`

**Rationale.** A closure payload cannot be cloned: an owning closure's environment has no duplicate. The explicit derive names the variant and payload that block it instead of synthesizing a clone() whose body would not compile.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cloneDeriveEnumClosurePayload.milo`](../tests/errors/cloneDeriveEnumClosurePayload.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `cannot derive Clone for 'Handle': it implements Drop`

**Rationale.** An enum with a Drop impl must never get clone(): both the clone and the original run drop, so the resource is released twice. Same message the struct derive gives.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cloneDeriveEnumDrop.milo`](../tests/errors/cloneDeriveEnumDrop.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `cannot derive Clone for 'Q': field 'h' of type 'H' has no clone`

**Rationale.** An explicit derive still reports the field that blocks it after the auto fixpoint has run: a @noCopy struct is never auto-clonable.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cloneDeriveExplicitNoCopyField.milo`](../tests/errors/cloneDeriveExplicitNoCopyField.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-cloneDeriveOptionClosure

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `cannot derive Clone for 'Q': field 'f' of type 'Option<move (i32) => i32>' has no clone`

**Rationale.** Option<closure> is a legal type with no clone(): an owning closure's environment has no duplicate, so the instance gets no conditional Clone. A struct holding one is silently non-clonable (Plain below compiles); asking for the derive names the field.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/cloneDeriveOptionClosure.milo`](../tests/errors/cloneDeriveOptionClosure.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-closureInferNoHint

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

### MILO-E-closureMoveInLoopViaCall

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `cannot move 'needle' out of a loop`

**Rationale.** A `move` closure built inside a loop takes its captures with it, so the second iteration has nothing left to capture. That was already rejected when the body USED the capture directly (`needle.len`), and silently accepted when the body passed it to a function instead — one operation, two spellings, two answers. Accepted, it did not merely compile: every iteration after the first captured an empty string and the program printed 4, 0, 0. A wrong answer with no diagnostic is the worst outcome available, which is what makes this an error fixture rather than a lint.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/closureMoveInLoopViaCall.milo`](../tests/errors/closureMoveInLoopViaCall.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-closureStoredInStruct

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `cannot store a closure that captures 'n' by reference`

**Rationale.** A closure without `move` captures by reference, so it is only valid while the frame owning the capture is alive. Returning one directly is fine (the checker promotes it to `move`), but hiding it inside a struct escaped by a side door the return check never saw: the struct is what leaves, and the closure rode along pointing at the dead frame. It read garbage at -O0 and hung at -O2, with ASAN silent because the capture lives on the stack. The store is rejected instead — `move` is the way to say you want the closure to own `n` (tests/fixtures/closureStoredMove.milo).

*Program:* [`tests/errors/closureStoredInStruct.milo`](../tests/errors/closureStoredInStruct.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-closureStoredInVec

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `cannot store a closure that captures 'n' by reference`

**Rationale.** The collection form of the same escape: pushing a by-reference closure into a Vec that then leaves the function. Rejected at the push — whether the Vec escapes is exactly what the checker cannot know without escape analysis, so it assumes it does.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/closureStoredInVec.milo`](../tests/errors/closureStoredInVec.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-coalesceMovesDefault

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `use of moved variable 'd'`

**Rationale.** `??` moves its default operand whether or not its result is bound. Recording that only when the result landed in a move position meant `(o ?? d).len` moved `d` with no diagnostic, and the later read of `d` printed an empty string.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/coalesceMovesDefault.milo`](../tests/errors/coalesceMovesDefault.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

**Rationale.** An alias is expanded, so one that names itself has no type to expand into and the expansion does not terminate. ### MILO-E-forInElementAssign

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `because 'v' is being iterated`

**Rationale.** `v.push(x)` inside the loop was rejected and `v[0] = x` was not — one rule, two answers depending on how the mutation was spelled. The assignment path exempts any index-qualified target because an in-place element write never reallocates, so a live slice VIEW stays valid and simply observes it. That reasoning does not extend to a loop: the loop is handing out that element, so rewriting it changes what the current binding names. Fixed by recording WHY a variable is frozen (view vs iteration) and applying the exemption only to views. Not a use-after-free — the slot does not move, so the loop reads the new value rather than freed memory. It is an iterator-invalidation semantics bug, and it is here because the inconsistency is what lets the next one through.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/forInElementAssign.milo`](../tests/errors/forInElementAssign.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

**Rationale.** The void guard used to live on the annotation path only, so a type argument the checker INFERRED slipped past it: `identity(nothing())` monomorphized at void and emitted `call void @identity_void(void void)`, which LLVM rejects at the link step against a temp .ll file with no source location. The guard now sits in monomorphizeFn, where every instantiation passes, spelled or inferred.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/genericVoidArgInferred.milo`](../tests/errors/genericVoidArgInferred.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-globalBorrowArgRealloc

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `and is passed a reference into 'G' here`

**Rationale.** No loop at all: the argument borrows G's storage and the callee reallocs G while that borrow is live, so the reference dangles before the callee is done reading it.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/globalBorrowArgRealloc.milo`](../tests/errors/globalBorrowArgRealloc.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-globalForInAcrossYield

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `'schedulerYield' can park this task while the loop variable is a reference into 'g's buffer`

**Rationale.** A for-in binding is a reference into the global's buffer. `schedulerYield` parks this task; the writer task then pushes 100000 elements, the buffer reallocs, and the next iteration reads freed memory. This program printed `reader sees 4528` before the rule. The fix is to iterate by index, snapshot with `.clone()`, or move the global into a task-owned value (see tests/fixtures/globalIndexLoopAcrossYield.milo).

*Program:* [`tests/errors/globalForInAcrossYield.milo`](../tests/errors/globalForInAcrossYield.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-globalForInParkTwoDeep

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `'step' can park this task while the loop variable is a reference into 'g's buffer`

**Rationale.** The may-park summary is transitive over the call graph: the loop body calls `step`, `step` calls `pause`, `pause` yields. The error names the call the loop body made, which is where the fix goes.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/globalForInParkTwoDeep.milo`](../tests/errors/globalForInParkTwoDeep.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-globalForInParkUnannotatedWrapper

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `'yieldNow' can park this task while the loop variable is a reference into 'g's buffer`

**Rationale.** `@parks` is a declared marker, not the mechanism. The summary walks the call graph down to the runtime leaves, so a std-style wrapper that forgets the attribute is still seen as parking: this `yieldNow` has no `@parks` and calls `schedulerYield` two levels down through helpers that have none either.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/globalForInParkUnannotatedWrapper.milo`](../tests/errors/globalForInParkUnannotatedWrapper.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-keyExtractorUserCombinator

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `cannot move 'u.name' out of the borrowed 'u'`

**Rationale.** `v.sortByKey((u: &User) => u.name)` compiles; this identical closure handed to a user's own non-retaining combinator does not. That asymmetry looks like a missing generalization, and it is NOT — relaxing it is a use-after-free, proven under ASan (heap-use-after-free in `pick`, 2026-08-16). The reason is not retention. `pick` never stores the key: it compares it and lets it go. The problem is that letting it go IS a free — `let a = key(v[i])` binds an owned string, so it carries ordinary scope-end drop glue, and the buffer it drops belongs to the User the closure only borrowed. `sortByKey` is exempt because a builtin is the one kind of callee that can promise never to drop the key it was handed. So the property that would close this is not "does the callee retain the result" but "can the callee hold the result without owning it" — which needs a borrowed return, and second-class references cannot return one. Don't wire a retention analysis to this rule; see backlog Tier 1 #6.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/keyExtractorUserCombinator.milo`](../tests/errors/keyExtractorUserCombinator.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

**Rationale.** `map` RETAINS what its closure returns, so a closure handing back a field of its borrowed parameter built a Vec<string> aliasing the source elements' buffers, and both freed them: a live double-free abort (exit 133), not a hypothetical one. This is why the move-out-of-a-borrow exemption is keyed to `sortByKey` alone rather than to closures in general — sortByKey reads the key to compare it and never stores or drops it, and that property has to be proved per callee, not assumed. Write `u.name.clone()` here.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/mapMoveFieldOutOfBorrow.milo`](../tests/errors/mapMoveFieldOutOfBorrow.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-matchBindingAssign

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `is bound by a pattern of an immutable subject`

**Rationale.** Assigning to a match binding of a `&Node` subject is rejected. The hint names the two ways that work: match through a `&mut` subject (the payload then binds as a `&mut` view, tests/fixtures/matchMutPayload.milo) or rebuild the variant. It must not offer the generic "declare with 'var'" advice: `v` is a pattern binding and there is no `let` to change.

### MILO-E-matchCopyBindMutate

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `is a copy of the matched payload — the write would be discarded`

**Rationale.** A `&mut self` method on a copy-bound pattern binding used to compile and silently throw the write away: inside bump v==2, after the match v==1. The identical operation through a `&mut` fn arg was always rejected ("cannot pass immutable 'n' as a '&mut' argument"), so the two paths disagreed and the accepting one was wrong. Three things have to line up for this to be a real loss, and they all do here: - Ctr is all-i64, so it is Copy and the binding is a snapshot (a non-Copy payload is MOVED into the binding, which then owns it — that write is real); - the binding is by value, not a `&` view into the enum; - the subject `b` is a VARIABLE that outlives the arm, so the discard is observable. Matching a temporary (`match Child.spawn(...)`) is legal for exactly the opposite reason — see tests/fixtures/matchTempBindMutate.milo.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/matchCopyBindMutate.milo`](../tests/errors/matchCopyBindMutate.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `cannot assign to 'n' because it is borrowed`

**Rationale.** While a payload view from a match arm is alive, the subject it points into cannot be reassigned: `n = Node.Empty` would drop the string `s` still views. This held a use-after-free for `&` views too (ASan: global-buffer-overflow on the print).

### MILO-E-matchSubjectReadInArm

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `use of moved variable 'x'`

**Rationale.** Reading the match subject inside an arm that destructures a non-Copy payload used to compile and silently read zeroed memory: codegen zeroes the payload slot at ARM ENTRY (extractBindings), but the checker deferred the subject's move to the END of the whole match, so the arm body saw it as still-live. The failure mode was worse than "always false" — the enum TAG survives the zeroing, so a discriminant-only predicate still answered correctly while a payload-reading one silently didn't. The `_` arm is deliberately non-destructuring: it must stay legal to read the subject there, since nothing is zeroed on that path.

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

**Rationale.** A `move` closure whose body moves a capture out can only run once. Captures live in the environment's own slots, so handing one to a callee by value zeroes the slot it came from. **Rationale.** Rust's E0509. A Drop impl takes '&mut self' and is written against every field being present, so a type with one cannot be taken apart field-wise. ### MILO-E-nestedFixedArray

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `a nested fixed array`

**Rationale.** `MiloType` carries ONE `isArray` flag, so an array of an array has nowhere to record the inner one. The parser built the type from the inner type's NAME alone, which silently dropped it: this annotation was accepted and meant `[i64; 2]`. A flat literal satisfied it, `g[1][0]` reported "cannot index type i64" against a program that had written a 2D array, and a nested literal reached clang as a store of `[2 x i64]` into an `i64` slot. An annotation the language cannot represent now says so.

### MILO-E-resultMapConsumesNonCopyErr

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `use of moved variable 'r'`

**Rationale.** map forwards the Err payload into its result untouched. With a non-Copy E that would leave the receiver and the result both owning one heap buffer, and both run drop glue — an ASAN double-free. So map consumes the receiver when E is non-Copy, and touching it afterwards must be rejected here rather than crashing at runtime. The error is built with pushStr, not a literal: a literal string has cap == 0, marking a static buffer that drop glue skips, so a literal payload would not expose the bug this rule exists to prevent.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/resultMapConsumesNonCopyErr.milo`](../tests/errors/resultMapConsumesNonCopyErr.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-resultOrElseOkMismatch

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `'orElse': callback's ok type must be i64, got string`

**Rationale.** orElse forwards the receiver's Ok payload verbatim on the short-circuit path, so there is nowhere to convert it. Only the ERROR type is free to change — that is the whole difference from andThen, which pins E and lets T vary.

### MILO-E-shardStringRejected

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `'parallelMap<string>' is not allowed: 'parallelMap' is @copyOnly and 'string' is not a Copy type (it owns heap memory)`

**Rationale.** `Shard.get` reads `self.base[i]` through a raw pointer: a bitwise copy of the element. For a `string` that is a second owner of one heap block, and this program (the 2026-09 soundness sweep's hole 1) freed it twice: once when `a` went out of scope and again when the reassembled Vec was dropped, a malloc abort at HEAD. The element type is decided where `parallelMap` is called, so that is where it is refused.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/shardStringRejected.milo`](../tests/errors/shardStringRejected.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-shardUseAfterMap

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `use of moved variable 'data'`

**Rationale.** parallelMap CONSUMES the Vec. That is the whole aliasing argument: while the buffer is divided there is no binding through which it can be reached except the disjoint windows, so nothing the workers touch can alias anything the caller still holds.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/shardUseAfterMap.milo`](../tests/errors/shardUseAfterMap.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-shardsManualPathPrivate

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `'shatter' is private to shard.milo`

**Rationale.** H2 from docs/plans/soundness-sweep-2026-09.md. This program divided a Vec by hand, handed a window to a worker and returned, dropping the `Shards` owner while the worker still wrote through the window: a heap-use-after-free under --sanitize that no checker rule could see. The fix is that the manual cycle no longer exists outside std/shard: `shatter`, `windows` and `weld` are module-private, and the only way to divide a buffer is a closed form (`parallelMap`, `parallelMapWith`, `parallelScanStr`) that awaits every worker before the owner can go away.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/shardsManualPathPrivate.milo`](../tests/errors/shardsManualPathPrivate.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-sharedMutAcrossThreads

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `use of moved variable`

**Rationale.** The parallel-sum footgun — the thing people get wrong. In C this compiles and silently races: long total = 0; // in each worker thread: total += partial; // torn reads, lost updates, passes tests "usually" In Milo you physically cannot hand the same mutable counter to two threads: the first `move` closure takes ownership of `total`, so the second capture is a use-after-move. The compiler names the fix — clone the handle (AtomicI64.clone shares one synchronized cell) or don't share at all and return partials. Either way the data race is unrepresentable, not merely discouraged. See tests/fixtures/battleConcurrency.milo for the guided-correct version.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/sharedMutAcrossThreads.milo`](../tests/errors/sharedMutAcrossThreads.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-sliceOfTemporary

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `cannot take a view of a temporary`

**Rationale.** A view of a temporary has nothing to freeze: `mk()[0..2]` points into a Vec that no binding owns. `freezeViewSource` already rejected the METHOD spelling of this (`mk().view()`), and its own comment is the reason the slice spelling had to be rejected too: that storage survives only because temporaries leak, and it becomes a use-after-free the moment they get drop glue. Three drop-glue paths landed on 2026-08-16 alone, so the gap between the two spellings was closing from the wrong side. One hazard, three spellings, and only one of them errored. See tests/errors/ stringSliceOfTemporary.milo for the string half.

*Program:* [`tests/errors/sliceOfTemporary.milo`](../tests/errors/sliceOfTemporary.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `errors (type checker rejects)`

### MILO-E-slicePush

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

- The diagnostic shall contain: `type '[i64]' has no method 'push'`

**Rationale.** A slice is a non-owning VIEW. The read-only combinators are available on it because it shares the Vec representation, but the mutating half of that same set must not be: a slice does not own the storage it points at, and growing it would reallocate a buffer belonging to someone else. Whitelisting the read-only names rather than widening the whole arm is what keeps this rejected.

A conforming implementation shall reject this program at compile time.

**Rationale.** Spawning transfers the closure: an owning closure is not Copy, so `Task.spawn(f)` moves it, `spawnWithStack` forgets it after handing the environment to the task, and the task releases it when it is reaped. **Rationale.** The exact declaration that cost node-milo hours: fcntl(fd, F_SETFL, flags) with fixed arity compiles clean, but on AArch64 the variadic callee reads its third arg off the stack while a fixed-arity call passes it in a register — so O_NONBLOCK never landed and every socket in the runtime stayed blocking. **Rationale.** A combinator lends its callback a pointer INTO the container it is iterating. Declaring the parameter by value is fine for a Copy element (codegen loads it), but a string owns heap: taking it by value would move it out of a container the caller still owns. Codegen passed the pointer anyway and the callback read it as a string, so `each` printed whatever bytes followed that address in the process — a memory disclosure out of ordinary safe Milo, no `unsafe` anywhere.

## Behavioural requirements

Programs a conforming implementation shall compile and run. Output is compared line by line after trimming, and is the whole of the requirement — a program that produces these lines by a different route conforms.

### MILO-B-addressOf

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `42`

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/addressOf.milo`](../tests/fixtures/addressOf.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-adoptDropRuns

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `made`
- `before`
- `dropped hello`
- `after`

**Rationale.** The adopted value is owned in FULL, not merely typed as owned: its drop runs, at the scope end the move checker picked, and it runs the whole way down. Three things are being asserted at once. The `impl Drop` print proves the user destructor fired on a value the compiler never saw allocated. Its position between "before" and "after" proves the drop is at the end of the block that owns the box, which is where any other `Heap<T>` would drop. And the `label` field is an owned string, so the drop glue that frees the box also frees a buffer one level down: the case an `extern struct` of raw pointers deliberately does NOT get (a raw pointer is not owned, so nothing under it is freed; see docs/foreign-memory.md). That the box itself is freed is not visible from Milo (freeing is not observable, only its absence is. It is gated from outside instead: scripts/leak-check.ts fails if this program exits still holding the allocation, and scripts/asan-sweep.ts fails if the free happens twice or the memory is read after it.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/adoptDropRuns.milo`](../tests/fixtures/adoptDropRuns.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-adoptExternStructFields

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `images=3`
- `still there: 1 2 3`
- `teardown done`

**Rationale.** What `adopt` does NOT reach, made observable. This is the giflib shape: an extern struct whose payload is a C-visible `(ptr, count)` pair. A raw pointer is not owned, so it gets no drop glue, so dropping the adopted `Heap<GifLike>` frees THE STRUCT and nothing it points at. That is correct: it is what makes the layout an ABI match. But it means `adopt` alone does not recursively free a C-shaped object graph, and a port has to walk the raw fields itself. The proof is the read AFTER the box has dropped: `savedImages` still holds 1 2 3. If dropping the box had freed the array, that read is a use-after-free and the `free` below is a double free, which is what scripts/asan-sweep.ts sees. The explicit teardown is the thing a giflib port would have to write.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/adoptExternStructFields.milo`](../tests/fixtures/adoptExternStructFields.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-adoptNull

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `none`
- `none`
- `none`
- `freed once`

**Rationale.** Null answers `Option.None` and adopts nothing, which is the only answer that can be right: there is no allocation to own, and a `Heap<T>` holding null would free null at scope exit. The null path is in the return type so it cannot be forgotten, exactly as `withRaw` puts it there. A negative length takes the same path for the same reason `withRaw` folds it in, with one addition: `withRaw` would have produced a view whose length field is nonsense, while `adoptSlice` would produce an OWNED value whose length field is nonsense, and its drop would then free elements that were never there. The buffer behind the rejected negative-length call is still the caller's, and this program frees it exactly once. Nothing adopted it; that is what is being checked.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/adoptNull.milo`](../tests/fixtures/adoptNull.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-adoptRoundTrip

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `7 9`
- `adopted 7 9`
- `3 30`

**Rationale.** The C-ABI round trip in both directions: a Milo allocation leaves through a raw pointer with `forget` (nothing will free it), C hands the same pointer back, and `adopt` reconstitutes an owned `Heap<T>` whose contents are the ones written before the trip. `adoptSlice` does the same for a run of elements. The box pointer comes out of a `Heap<T>` with `h.ptr()`, the sibling of `v.ptr()`. This fixture keeps the older double indirection through `h.addrOf()` — the address of the SLOT holding the pointer, one load above the pointer itself — because both spellings must keep working and `heapPtrGive.milo` proves they are the same address.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/adoptRoundTrip.milo`](../tests/fixtures/adoptRoundTrip.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-adoptSliceOwned

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `len=4 cap=4`
- `11 22 33 44`
- `sum=110`
- `len=5 last=55`
- `extended=4 7 8`
- `empty len=0`

**Rationale.** `adoptSlice` hands back a real `Vec<T>`, not a view: every element reads back, the length is the one that was asked for, and the value is growable: a `push` past the adopted extent is the operation a slice is denied, and it works here because this Vec owns its buffer. Capacity is `len`, not the 0 that marks a non-owning view, and both halves of that are checked. `capacity()` reads the field directly, which is what a slice has no method for and is why feature 1's cap could not be gated at all. The `extend` is the other half: `genVecExtend` frees its SOURCE buffer only when the source capacity is above zero, so an adopted Vec consumed by `extend` is freed there, and with cap 0 it would silently leak, which scripts/leak-check.ts is what sees. A `len == 0` adoption is still an owned, empty Vec that frees its pointer at scope exit, which is what a zero-length Milo allocation is.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/adoptSliceOwned.milo`](../tests/fixtures/adoptSliceOwned.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-aesGcmLengthGuards

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `roundtrip=[secret msg]`
- `short-key rejected`
- `short-tag rejected`

**Rationale.** The AES-GCM entry points pass 32/16/12 to the C API as CONSTANTS, whatever length actually arrived. `requires key.len == 32` states that, but a `requires` is asserted in --debug and DROPPED at the default -O2 — so a short key read past the end of the caller's buffer in an ordinary build and "encrypted" with whatever followed it. An out-of-bounds read reachable from safe Milo through a pub std API. Skipped on Windows because that arm has no AES to guard: the MSVC CRT provides no C-linkable AES-GCM, so the std/cryptosys.windows arm fails by design (aesUnsupported). There is no pointer handed to C there, and so no length to check. The lengths are checked at runtime now, so the guard holds in a release build. Both bad cases here are derived at RUNTIME on purpose: a literal short key is caught by the prover at compile time, which proves the contract works but not that the release build is safe, which is the thing that was broken.

### MILO-B-aliasBehindRef

### MILO-B-arenaRead

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `true`
- `42`
- `false`
- `42`

**Rationale.** `Arena.read` — the borrowing read, and the reason it exists. `a.get(h)` returns `Option<T>` by value, which for any T that owns heap is an allocation on EVERY read: measured at 20ms per 200k reads of the node below, against ~0 for this path. That gap is not academic — it is why real programs (milojs's whole object graph is `JSValue.Obj(i64)`, a bare index into a Vec) reach past generational handles for an unchecked integer. The blessed idiom has to be the fast one or it does not get used. `arenaWith` is the more natural spelling and cannot be a method: its result type is a parameter of the METHOD rather than of Arena, and a method's own type parameter is never inferred at the call site. `read` returns bool and takes the value out through a captured var, so it has no such parameter and gets a method form today.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/arenaRead.milo`](../tests/fixtures/arenaRead.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-arrayCombinators

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `10`
- `2`
- `20`
- `true`
- `true`
- `10`
- `10`

**Rationale.** The combinators on a FIXED array, and a fixed array passed to a slice parameter. Slices got these first by re-tagging: `&[T]` already carries the `%Vec` layout, so the existing emitters worked unchanged. A fixed array cannot be re-tagged — `[N x T]` is an inline layout, not a `{ptr,len,cap}` view — so both halves here materialise a full-range slice, which is exactly the `VecSlice` node `a[0..N]` already produced. That is why this needed no new emitter either, only the conversion at the two boundaries: a.sum() — lowering wraps the receiver total(a) — the checker marks the argument, lowering wraps it The second was the visible asymmetry: `total(v)` on a Vec worked and `total(a)` on an array did not, for the same function and the same element type. `clone` is deliberately NOT in the array set: cloning a fixed array should yield an array, and re-tagging would quietly change the result type to a slice.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/arrayCombinators.milo`](../tests/fixtures/arrayCombinators.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-arrayCopy

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `7`
- `7`
- `4`
- `4`
- `1`

**Rationale.** A fixed-size array of Copy elements is itself Copy (Rust: `[T; N]: Copy where T: Copy`). Before this, `[u8; 16]` — an IPv6 address — could not be handed to two functions: the first call moved it, and the compiler's hint said to "clone it at the point of transfer", which arrays have no method for. The diagnostic named a fix that could not be applied. The element check is what keeps this honest: an array of non-Copy elements still moves. See tests/errors/arrayNonCopyMove.milo for that half.

### MILO-B-asyncCallOrdering

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `body-start caller-continues body-resumed`

**Rationale.** The ordering an async call needs, expressed with green tasks: JS runs an async body synchronously up to its first `await`, and only then does the call return to the caller. Task.spawn alone will not do it — a queued task does not start until the next scheduler turn, so the caller would run first. Park and unpark are enough: the caller spawns the body then parks, and the body unparks the caller at its first suspension point before parking itself. No scheduler primitive is needed, which is what keeps await suspension inside milojs rather than in the task struct that channels, select and net all share.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/asyncCallOrdering.milo`](../tests/fixtures/asyncCallOrdering.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-bandParallelBuffer

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `rows 64`
- `identical true`
- `sum 16773120`

**Rationale.** Band-parallel writes into ONE shared buffer, which is how a software rasteriser uses more than one core (examples/games/flight/raster.milo). The shape under test: N OS threads each own a disjoint, contiguous range of rows of a single Vec<f32>. A worker cannot be handed a `&mut [f32]` window — second- class references do not cross a thread boundary, and the runtime-`n` `splitMut` that would carve one buffer into `n` of them is unbuilt (docs/roadmap.md) — so the base address rides across as an i64 and the worker re-derives the pointer. The check is the one that matters for a band split: the parallel result must be BIT-IDENTICAL to the serial one. A band boundary that is off by a row shows up as a handful of wrong pixels, which a tolerance-based comparison would pass.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/bandParallelBuffer.milo`](../tests/fixtures/bandParallelBuffer.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-base32Strict

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

**Rationale.** Base32.decode is strict RFC 4648; Base32.decodeLoose is the human-transcribed- secret path. Both reject non-alphabet bytes — leniency covers formatting only. Previously every failing line here silently skipped the offending byte and returned a shorter secret, which HOTP then turned into wrong-but-valid codes.

### MILO-B-battleConcurrency

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `80000`
- `80000`
- `80000`
- `7`
- `ok`

**Rationale.** Concurrency battle-test — sum-conservation under real multicore contention. This is the GUIDED-CORRECT counterpart to tests/errors/sharedMutAcrossThreads.milo: you cannot hand one mutable counter to many threads (that's a use-after-move), so each worker clone()s its own handle to the shared synchronized cell. The naive racy version simply does not compile. WORKERS OS threads (Promise.blocking, genuine parallelism) each hammer a shared AtomicI64, race a CAS loop, and stream a partial through a Channel. Execution is thoroughly racy; three INDEPENDENT tallies must agree exactly, so a lost atomic update, dropped/duplicated message, cross-thread UAF, or double-free of a shared refcounted cell breaks the invariant instead of passing silently.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/battleConcurrency.milo`](../tests/fixtures/battleConcurrency.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-bigAggSret

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `11`
- `99`
- `22`
- `105`
- `107`

**Rationale.** Big aggregates (≥128B) must never become first-class LLVM SSA values — a whole-aggregate load/store gets SROA-expanded per-element with no size cap, which made -O2 builds of struct-heavy code (NES %Bus/%Ppu framebuffer) never finish. Codegen lowers big-agg returns to sret + moves/copies to llvm.memcpy. This exercises constructor-return, big-agg copy, and copy independence.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/bigAggSret.milo`](../tests/fixtures/bigAggSret.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

### MILO-B-blanketImpl

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `[1,2]`
- `["a"]`
- `[[1],[2]]`

**Rationale.** `impl Trait for Vec<T>` — a blanket impl on a builtin container. These parsed and landed in the generic-impl table under the bare name "Vec", and nothing ever instantiated them: `monomorphizeStruct` is what instantiates a generic impl, and a builtin container is not a struct, so it was never called. The call then died in Vec's own method arm, which errors before anything consults a trait impl — `Vec has no method 'toJ'` on a program that plainly defines one. They are instantiated per ELEMENT type under a mangled name (`Vec_i64`), which is what keeps two of them apart: `resolveMethod` is keyed by a bare type name, so without the mangling a `Vec<i64>` impl and a `Vec<string>` impl are the same key. Both are exercised below for exactly that reason.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/blanketImpl.milo`](../tests/fixtures/blanketImpl.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-blanketImplHashMap

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `keys=3 vals=60`
- `keys=2 vals=3`

**Rationale.** `impl Trait for HashMap<K, V>` — the two-parameter case of a blanket impl on a builtin container. Vec's version shipped first; the map arm had the identical hole (it errored with `HashMap has no method '…'` before anything consulted a trait impl) and is closed the same way, instantiated per key/value pair under a mangled name. The body uses BOTH parameters, which is the point: substitution has to bind K and V to the right positions, and an impl whose parameter count does not match the container's is skipped rather than bound wrongly. Output is deliberately order-independent — HashMap iteration order is unspecified, so a fixture that printed entries in traversal order would be a flake, not a test.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/blanketImplHashMap.milo`](../tests/fixtures/blanketImplHashMap.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-blockScopeDrop

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `-bd--|end`

**Rationale.** Block-scope drop: an owned value dies at the end of the innermost block that owns it, not at the function epilogue. Here `t` is declared inside an `if` that runs only on iteration 1; it drops at the end of that if-block ("d" lands right after "b", before the trailing "-"). Before block-scope drop it would have lived until the function epilogue, printing "-b--d|end". Total drop count is unchanged (guarded/idempotent) — only the drop's program point moves earlier, which is the whole point.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/blockScopeDrop.milo`](../tests/fixtures/blockScopeDrop.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-blockValueOwnership

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `if-temps 1244000`
- `match-temps 1240000`
- `mixed 620040`
- `locals 1244000`
- `scalar 280`

**Rationale.** An `if`/`match` used as an EXPRESSION hands back whatever its arms produce. When every arm produces a value nobody else owns, so does the expression — and nothing was freeing it: `(if c { big(i) } else { big(j) }).len` leaked the whole string every evaluation. The test is an AND across arms, and it deliberately does NOT accept an arm that is a plain variable. `??` does accept one, because it MOVES out of the branch it takes and zeroes the source; an `if` arm does not, so `if c { a } else { b }` for locals a and b leaves both still owned by the enclosing scope. Dropping the result on top of that is a double free — it aborts with exit 133 — which is why the `locals` case below is here: it must keep working, and it is already correct because the scope frees a and b. ~16 KB strings on purpose; `leaks` does not report a small abandoned block, because it stays reachable from a stale stack slot. See tests/fixtures/closureParamOwnership.milo.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/blockValueOwnership.milo`](../tests/fixtures/blockValueOwnership.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-borrowIndexFieldPrecision

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `3`
- `13`
- `2`

**Rationale.** Iterating `self.items[i].ids` must not freeze all of `self`. The index makes the path imprecise about WHICH element, but the path to the container — `items` — is exact, so a call that mutates a sibling field (`self.log`) touches provably disjoint storage and has to be allowed. milo-self used to collapse the whole access path to the bare root at the first index step, so the loop below froze `self` entirely and `self.log.record(..)` was rejected as "cannot call 'record' on 'self' because it is borrowed". That is what kept examples/tools/java-dap from type-checking. The freeze is still a superset of the real borrow: anything reached through `self.items` conflicts (see borrowIndexFieldAliases in tests/errors), only siblings are freed.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/borrowIndexFieldPrecision.milo`](../tests/fixtures/borrowIndexFieldPrecision.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-boundsCheckShadowedVec

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `first=0 second=7 third=14`
- `total=21`

**Rationale.** A loop's hoisted length must come from the vec the body actually indexes. Both locals here are named `saves`: the first is bound on a branch this call does not take, the second inside the loop. The preheader resolves the name in the enclosing scope, so keying the hoisted length on the name alone checked every `saves[i]` in the loop against the OTHER local's length — zero here, so a valid index trapped 0/0. Found by milojs's regex engine (`String.match` with /g), whose regexStrMatch has exactly this pair of same-named locals.

**Rationale.** this fixture declares its own POSIX extern (clock_gettime), which the MSVC CRT does not provide — a test-side portability limit, not a compiler gap. @cLayout verifies a declared extern-struct layout against the real C header at build time. `struct timespec` is two 64-bit fields on every target Milo hosts, so a correct declaration must build and run. The mismatch cases live in tests/errors/cLayout*.milo.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/cLayout.milo`](../tests/fixtures/cLayout.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-cLayoutOpaque

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `true`
- `true`
- `true`

**Rationale.** struct timeval's tv_usec is suseconds_t, which is i32 on macOS but i64 on 64-bit Linux. This fixture declares tv_usec: i32 (+ i32 opaque padding), which is the macOS layout; on Linux @cLayout correctly rejects it as not matching the header. The @cOpaque feature under test is platform-independent — only this particular struct's layout is macOS-specific — so the test runs on macOS and is skipped on Linux rather than asserting a wrong layout there. Windows is skipped for a different reason: it has no <sys/time.h> at all, so the guard TU fails to compile before any layout claim is even evaluated. @cOpaque marks a field as filler with no C counterpart, so @cLayout skips it. Needed for structs padded out to a size C dictates: node-milo's `Rusage` names only ru_utime/ru_stime but must be 144 bytes because that is what getrusage() writes, so it pads with _p0.._pD. Those fields do not exist in `struct rusage`, so @cLayout emitted `offsetof(struct rusage, _p0)` — ill-formed C — and the struct could not be verified at all. The prefix rule covers an omitted *tail*, not opaque padding in the middle. The real fields must still be checked; `struct timeval` is the same shape as Rusage's leading members, so this pins both halves: named fields verified, filler ignored, and the size assert still counts the filler (that is the point of declaring it).

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/cLayoutOpaque.milo`](../tests/fixtures/cLayoutOpaque.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-cSig

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `true`
- `true`
- `true`

**Rationale.** this fixture declares its own POSIX extern (sysconf), which the MSVC CRT does not provide — a test-side portability limit, not a compiler gap. @cSig verifies an extern fn's signature against the real C header at build time. `sysconf` is the case that motivated the design: it returns `long`, and on macOS `int64_t` is `long long` — a distinct type of the same width. So the compiler cannot derive the C signature from the Milo one; the declaration states it and the build checks it. Mismatch cases live in tests/errors/cSig*.milo.

**Rationale.** this fixture declares POSIX externs (time, uname) that the MSVC CRT does not provide the same way — a test-side portability limit, not a compiler gap. @cSig verifies a `?&mut T` parameter against the real header, and it verifies clean because the C type genuinely IS `T *`: the nullable spelling adds no tag and no wrapper. `time` is the case that motivated the design — a C API whose null argument is a documented mode, not an error — and `uname` covers the struct pointee, where the guard compares sizeof(*(struct utsname *)0) against the Milo struct's size.

- `true`

- `true`

**Rationale.** @cValue verifies an integer constant against the C macro it transcribes. @cSig covers functions and @cLayout covers structs; a bare constant had no anchor at all, so a wrong scancode or pixel format was a runtime bug — a dead key, a garbled frame — with no link error and no diagnostic. Mismatch cases live in tests/errors/cValue*.milo. These use libc headers so the guard is real on every CI platform. The motivating case is a third-party lib (SDL's SDL_PIXELFORMAT_ABGR8888 = 0x16762004, transcribed by hand in a dozen places), which needs pkg-config to find its headers.

- `task got 7`
- `sender resumed`

**Rationale.** cross-world wakes both directions: a foreign OS-thread send unparks a green task blocked in recv (via the scheduler wakeup event), and a foreign-thread recv unparks a green task blocked in send on a full channel. The OS threads come from Promise.blocking (fire-and-forget — they finish before the tasks they wake, so their handles are unused).

### MILO-B-channelDropPayload

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `empty: none`
- `sent: true`
- `id: 7`
- `via peer: hello`
- `handles: ok`

**Rationale.** A channel payload that itself owns a Drop type. send/trySend memcpy the value into the buffer and memset the local instead of forgetting it, and recv/tryRecv build a `zeroed<T>()` that drops untouched on every early return, so every std handle has to drop to nothing when its pointer is zero. Before that guard, a struct holding a Channel segfaulted on the send, and tryRecv on an empty channel segfaulted too.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/channelDropPayload.milo`](../tests/fixtures/channelDropPayload.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-channelDropsUndelivered

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `recv 1`
- `after recv: made 3 gone 1`
- `wraparound: made 4 gone 4`
- `peer dropped: made 2 gone 0`
- `clone first: made 4 gone 4`

**Rationale.** Payloads still queued when the last Channel owner drops are destroyed, once each. Before this, ChannelHandle's Drop freed the ring buffer without running T's drop glue on the slots between head and tail, so every undelivered Drop value leaked (soundness sweep H6). The received value belongs to the receiver and drops on its own scope exit, so it must be counted exactly once, not again by the channel.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/channelDropsUndelivered.milo`](../tests/fixtures/channelDropsUndelivered.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-channelMainDrivesScheduler

- `got 10`
- `got 20`
- `got 30`

**Rationale.** Regression: main thread calls ch.recv() directly (not inside a spawned task) with only a green sender. Before the three-branch fix, recv fell through to pthread_cond_wait and deadlocked — nobody was driving the scheduler, so the green sender never ran. Now recv drives the scheduler itself when a scheduler exists on the main thread.

### MILO-B-channelMainSendDrivesScheduler

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `got 1`
- `got 2`
- `got 3`
- `got 4`
- `main done`

**Rationale.** Regression (symmetric to channelMainDrivesScheduler): main thread calls ch.send() directly into a cap-1 channel with only a green receiver. When the buffer is full, send must drive the scheduler so the green receiver can drain it — a bare pthread_cond_wait would deadlock. The final done.recv() (also from main) blocks until the receiver has consumed every item, making the output deterministic under Go exit semantics.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/channelMainSendDrivesScheduler.milo`](../tests/fixtures/channelMainSendDrivesScheduler.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

**Rationale.** A hand-written impl Clone on an enum is honoured by the struct that holds it: the struct's auto-derived clone() calls the user's method (which prints) exactly once per clone, and the enum is never given a second, synthesized clone. ### MILO-B-closureArgEnvOwnership

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `hello`
- `hello`
- `hi`
- `5`
- `5`

**Rationale.** Who frees the heap environment of a `move` closure passed as an argument. The caller does, right after the call, because neither side otherwise can: an owning fn type is not Copy, so passing the closure ends the caller's own drop of it, and the callee's parameter is declared with the non-owning `(T) => R`, which the checker treats as Copy — so a callee that forwarded it twice would free the same environment twice. That is why the caller keeps ownership and the rule is "free after the call unless the closure can outlive it" (codegen: closureParamEscapes). Each shape below is one arm of that rule. The leak side is pinned by tests/leak-clean.txt (closureCaptureHeap, closureCaptureMutableLocal); the double-free side is pinned here, because a second free of an environment is a crash, not a wrong number.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/closureArgEnvOwnership.milo`](../tests/fixtures/closureArgEnvOwnership.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-closureCaptureDropMatrix

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `neverCalled 1`
- `calledOnce 1`
- `consumed 1`
- `passed 1`
- `returned 1`
- `twoCapsOneConsumed 2`
- `nested 1`
- `loop3 3`

**Rationale.** Every shape a `move` closure's captures can travel through, counted. A destructor that never runs leaks nothing when the value owns no heap, so `leaks` cannot see it and neither can ASan. Counting Drop runs is the only oracle that can, and every id here is ZERO on purpose: an all-zero capture is a live value whose destructor must still run, and a version of the env drop glue that sniffed slots for all-zero to decide "already moved out" skipped exactly these and nothing else. The two failures this pins, both found by sweeping the shapes rather than by a fixture anyone had written: - a zero-valued capture never destroyed (glue read the value instead of a flag) - a capture re-captured by a NESTED move closure destroyed twice (the inner env took ownership, the outer env's flag was never cleared)

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/closureCaptureDropMatrix.milo`](../tests/fixtures/closureCaptureDropMatrix.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-closureCapturesDropped

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `290`
- `done`

**Rationale.** The closure drop glue. An owning closure holds a heap environment; slot 0 of that environment is its drop function, emitted per closure SHAPE because the capture list is known only where the closure is built — at the drop site all that is known is the type `move () => R`, which says a closure owns something but not what. Keeping the glue in the environment rather than in a third word of the pair is what leaves a closure two words wide, so nothing that passes one around had to change. On tests/leak-clean.txt: before this, every one of these leaked its captures. A SPAWNED closure is deliberately not exercised here — a task's environment is still leaked on purpose (std/runtime reapTask says why), so including one would take this off the manifest and lose the assertion that matters.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/closureCapturesDropped.milo`](../tests/fixtures/closureCapturesDropped.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

**Rationale.** The other side of the retention rule: a callee that only CALLS its fn-typed parameter does not keep it, so handing it a closure that captures by reference is safe and stays accepted. `g(1)` parses as a call with no Ident node for `g`, so "appears as an Ident anywhere in the body" is exactly "used as a value" — which is what makes this computable rather than annotated. `forward` pins the recursive half: a one-line wrapper keeps its argument only if what it forwards to does.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/closureNonRetainingArg.milo`](../tests/fixtures/closureNonRetainingArg.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-closureParamOwnership

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `local 3560200`
- `temp 2760200`
- `fold 110`

**Rationale.** A by-value owned parameter belongs to the CALLEE. A plain `fn` has always done that — its parameter gets an alive flag and a guarded drop at scope exit, skipped if the body moved the value out. A CLOSURE did not: it stored the parameter and registered a local and stopped there. The caller does not cover it either. It MOVES the value in — zeroes the slot, clears the alive flag — and then frees nothing. So nobody freed it, and every call through a closure leaked its owned argument. The strings here are ~16 KB on purpose. At small sizes `leaks` finds the abandoned block reachable from a stale stack slot and reports nothing, which is why 548 leak-gated fixtures never caught this. At this size it is 200 leaks per 200 calls, so the fixture has to allocate big enough for the oracle to see the failure it exists to catch.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/closureParamOwnership.milo`](../tests/fixtures/closureParamOwnership.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `6`
- `7`
- `20`

**Rationale.** The positive half of tests/errors/closureStoredInStruct.milo: `move` heap-owns the captures, so a closure stored in a struct or a Vec may outlive the frame it was written in. A capture-free closure needs no `move` — there is nothing to dangle.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/closureStoredMove.milo`](../tests/fixtures/closureStoredMove.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-defaultValueOwnership

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `ident 1560000`
- `none-default 1560000`
- `bound 1560000`
- `call 1560000`

**Rationale.** `??` MOVES out of whichever branch it takes, zeroing the source — so its result is owned and the named variable it came from will not be dropped by its scope either. The ownership test required every branch to be a literal or a temporary, which missed the commonest shape: an Option held in a variable. let o: Option<string> = Option.Some(big(i)) (o ?? "d").len // payload leaked, every evaluation Both branches leaked — Some through the payload, None through a computed default. ~16 KB strings on purpose: `leaks` finds a small abandoned block reachable from a stale stack slot and reports nothing, so this fixture has to allocate big enough for the oracle to see the failure it exists to catch. See tests/fixtures/closureParamOwnership.milo.

The self-hosted codegen had no assignment arm for a deref target, so every one of these silently lowered to nothing — no store, no diagnostic. std/ws built a whole frame header byte by byte into a buffer that stayed zeroed, and the reader then blocked forever waiting on bytes the writer believed it had sent.

### MILO-B-deriveJsonFixedArray

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `{"cells":[1,2,3],"name":"b","flags":[true,false]}`
- `rt 13btrue`
- `wrong length rejected`

**Rationale.** `@derive(Json)` on a fixed-array field. A Vec grows into, a fixed array cannot: it has to exist before it is filled, so decoding one needs both a zero value to build the slots with and an exact-length check. A wire array of the wrong length is REPORTED rather than truncated or zero-padded, because silently accepting it hands the caller a slot the sender never wrote.
