### MILO-B-deriveTemplate

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `Point<3>: x:i64=1 y:i64=2 label:string=here`
- `Point x,y,label`

**Rationale.** A user-defined `@derive(Trait)`. The generator is written in Milo, not in the compiler: `@derive(Json)` is 366 lines of TypeScript, which makes every new derive a compiler PR, and this is the extension point that removes that. Every hole is exercised: `@Self`/`@SelfStr` outside the repetition, `@count` (the field count as an integer literal), and inside `@fields` the field's name, its name as a string, its written type, its type as a string, and its index. Each also substitutes inside a string or f-string, which is what makes a template readable.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/deriveTemplate.milo`](../tests/fixtures/deriveTemplate.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-deriveTemplateJson

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `{"id":7,"name":"x","ok":true,"sub":{"n":3},"tags":["a","b"],"nums":[1,2]}`

**Rationale.** The dogfood case for user-defined derives: a complete, recursive JSON serializer written entirely in Milo, with no compiler change and no `src/derive-json.ts`. It works because dispatch does the type-directed part that a macro would otherwise have to do: the template body is uniform (`self.@name.toJ()`) and traits pick the right implementation per field. That is the same division of labour serde uses — the macro emits `self.field.serialize()` and the blanket impls carry the weight — and it is why the template language needs no conditionals and stays non-Turing-complete. **This used to stop at container fields.** `impl ToJ for Vec<T>` parsed and was never instantiated, so a `Vec<string>` field reported `Vec has no method 'toJ'`. Blanket impls on builtin containers close that (see tests/fixtures/blanketImpl.milo), and the `tags` and `nums` fields below are here to keep it closed.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/deriveTemplateJson.milo`](../tests/fixtures/deriveTemplateJson.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

Each block resets the counter, runs one shape, and prints the count.

### MILO-B-dropBehindTraitObject

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `sum 6`
- `dropA 1`
- `dropA 3`

**Rationale.** A value behind a boxed interface must still run its destructor. The concrete type is erased at the box, so the only route to its `Drop` impl is the itable — and the itable carried method pointers only. `Heap<Iface>` therefore freed the box and never destroyed what was inside it: RAII through polymorphism was silently a no-op, while the identical `Heap<Concrete>` was correct. Fixed by appending one slot to every itable holding the concrete type's destructor (or null when it has none), which is why `B` below prints nothing. Appended rather than prepended so existing method indices stay valid.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/dropBehindTraitObject.milo`](../tests/fixtures/dropBehindTraitObject.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-dropEmptyContainerField

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `made 1`
- `dropped 1`

**Rationale.** A struct's drop glue decides "was this moved out of and zeroed?" by probing a field's data pointer. An empty container defeats that probe: `Vec.new()` that never grew has a null data pointer and is perfectly alive, so a struct whose FIRST heap field was an empty Vec skipped its entire destructor — user `Drop` impl included — and silently never cleaned up. Found by tests/dropCounting.test.ts, which counts destructor runs instead of checking whether a program is accepted. The accept/reject suites cannot see a missing drop.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/dropEmptyContainerField.milo`](../tests/fixtures/dropEmptyContainerField.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

- `1000`
- `100`
- `7`

**Rationale.** A value bound by a match arm owns its payload, so it must be dropped when the arm exits — including via `return` from inside the arm, which previously dropped nothing at all (one leaked SSL_CTX per HTTPS request in milojs). A binding moved out of the arm must NOT be dropped: it now belongs elsewhere.

### MILO-B-dropZeroedFdSlot

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `fd0 preserved`

**Rationale.** Regression: drop glue runs on zeroed moved-from slots (the move-zero convention), so fd-holding Drop impls must treat fd 0 as "not owned" (`fd > 0`, not `>= 0`). A let-bound Result matched in a loop reassign-drops the previous iteration's (payload-zeroed) value each time; with the old `fd >= 0` guard that was close(0), so every 2nd+ accept()/open() in a plain loop returned the freed fd 0 — this killed multi-request TCP servers in the main context. devNull() (not a hard-coded "/dev/null") keeps this runnable on Windows, where the bit-bucket is spelled NUL — the fd-slot behaviour under test is OS-independent.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/dropZeroedFdSlot.milo`](../tests/fixtures/dropZeroedFdSlot.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-enumPayloadOptionLayout

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `true`
- `true`
- `true`
- `true`
- `true`

**Rationale.** An Option whose payload is itself a multi-slot enum must reserve room for the WHOLE payload enum — its tag word included — not just that enum's payload blob. Enums lower to `{ i32 tag, [slots x i64] }`. Getting this one slot short does not fail to compile and does not fail any test that only reads the Option back: the store simply runs 8 bytes past the end of the alloca and lands on whatever local the stack allocator put next. In the self-hosted compiler that neighbour was a loop counter, so `for j in 0..info.fields.len` resumed with a garbage j and tripped a bounds check thousands of iterations into an unrelated function. The assertions below are deliberately about a NEIGHBOURING local surviving, not about the Option's own value — an 8-byte overrun leaves the Option itself perfectly readable, which is why this class of bug hides. The payload types that matter are the GENERIC INSTANCES (Option<i64>). They are registered under a mangled name (Option_i64), so a sizing pass that measures the unmangled spelling finds no such enum and falls back to a bare word — 8 bytes for something that is really 16. Plain scalar payloads never expose this.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/enumPayloadOptionLayout.milo`](../tests/fixtures/enumPayloadOptionLayout.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `2`
- `169`
- `142`
- `-1`

**Rationale.** Integer-repr'd (C-like) enums: `enum Kind: i32 { ... }`. `k as i32` is always defined and yields the variant's discriminant — auto-assigned from 0, or explicit `= N` (sparse, and it resumes counting after an explicit value). Payload-free by construction; match still works.

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `text:hi`
- `empty`

**Rationale.** An enum built at a call site and passed to a `&T` parameter used to leak its payload: codegen registers argument temporaries for drop via isOwnedTempExpr, and EnumLit was not in that list, so the temp had no name for scope-drop to find and no entry in the temp registry. `renderResponse(Response.Text(body), hdrs)` in std/http lost `body` on every call. This fixture only pins that the program still prints the right bytes; the leak itself is caught by tests/leak-clean.txt, which is where the regression would show.

### MILO-B-enumTryFrom

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `true`
- `true`
- `true`
- `true`

**Rationale.** `Kind.tryFrom(n): Option<Kind>` — the partial reverse of `k as i32`. Some(variant) when n is a discriminant, None otherwise. It is DERIVED from the same discriminant list as the cast, so the round-trip law `tryFrom(k as i32) == Some(k)` holds by construction — there is no hand-written table to drift out of sync (the classic rot in sparse enums).

### MILO-B-escapingClosureMove

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `15`
- `heap-owned!`
- `bound!`

**Rationale.** A closure that escapes its defining frame must OWN its captures, and `move` is how you say so. This used to be automatic: the Return path silently promoted a returned closure to `move`. That promotion was unsound in a way the reject is not — it happened at the `return`, after the move checker had already walked the body, so a read of the capture between the closure and the return sailed past and printed empty (tests/errors/escapingClosureReturn.milo now holds that program). All three return shapes work with an explicit `move`: a captured parameter, a captured local, and a local bound first and returned by name.

**Rationale.** A C function-pointer FIELD in an extern struct: the ops-table shape every C library hands out. The field holds ONE word (the code pointer), not Milo's { code, environment } pair, so the layout matches C and the call passes no hidden argument. The three things a field like this admits are all here: fill it with a top-level fn, call through it, copy it to another field of the same type. Everything else is a tests/errors/externFnPtr*.milo rejection.

### MILO-B-externFnPtrFieldCLayout

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `true`
- `true`
- `true`
- `true`

**Rationale.** zlib.h is not part of the MSVC CRT, so the guard would be a named skip there rather than a check — a test-side portability limit, not a compiler gap. The layout claim a fn-pointer field makes, checked against a real C header instead of asserted. zlib's `z_stream` carries two of them (`zalloc`, `zfree`) in the middle of the struct, so a field lowered as Milo's fat { code, env } pair would push `zfree` and everything after it eight bytes out and the offsetof asserts would fail. This declares a PREFIX of z_stream, which @cLayout allows (the size assert is `>=`). Nothing here calls zlib, so no library is linked; only the header is read.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/externFnPtrFieldCLayout.milo`](../tests/fixtures/externFnPtrFieldCLayout.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-fieldCloneOutOfContainer

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `HandleOfString`
- `HandleOfString`
- `FirstOwned`
- `SecondOwned`

**Rationale.** A droppable field read out of a container element (`sig.params[i].ty`) is a COPY, not a move: the container keeps owning that field. Copying the header alone gives two owners of one buffer, and whichever drops first leaves the other dangling — milo-self's codegen did exactly that (the enclosing HashMap value and the local copy both freed the same TStruct name string, aborting in libmalloc while the compiler type-checked any method call with a struct-typed parameter). The reads are deliberately repeated: the first copy is dropped when its scope ends, so a shallow copy makes every later read of the same element see freed bytes.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/fieldCloneOutOfContainer.milo`](../tests/fixtures/fieldCloneOutOfContainer.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

Widening is locked in at the first read (see the adversarial error fixtures), so it can never retroactively change a committed i32 use.

- `0`
- `-123.456`
- `10000000000000000`
- `0.0001`
- `1e+21`
- `1e-07`
- `inf`
- `nan`
- `0.33333334`
- `0.3333333333333333 100`
- `Point { x: 0.3333333333333333, y: 100 }`
- `{"x":0.3333333333333333,"y":100}`

**Rationale.** Floats must print the shortest decimal that reads back as the same value. Before this, every float went through printf "%g" — 6 significant digits — so 1.0/3.0 printed as 0.333333 and 123456789.123456 as 1.23457e+08, silently losing data on the way into logs, jsonStringify and toString. The magnitude cases (100.0, 1e16) guard the other half: picking the shortest *precision* alone would render 100.0 as the round-tripping but unreadable "1e+02", because %g goes exponential once the exponent reaches the precision.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/floatRoundTrip.milo`](../tests/fixtures/floatRoundTrip.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-flybyGeometry

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `fold roundtrip ok`
- `wrapPi ok`
- `ring walk ok`
- `parsed Town 1 building 1 bridge`
- `truncations rejected 141/141`
- `bad magic rejected`
- `huge grid rejected`

**Rationale.** Locks the pure geometry and the asset decoder the FLYBY example is built on. The example's own harness (examples/games/flight/shot.milo) renders frames but asserts nothing, so none of this was covered: a mirrored-map bug, a ring-walk that skips a tile, or a decoder that aborts on a short file all used to be invisible until something looked wrong on screen. SKIPPED where the `gl` package is not installed. This fixture reaches it without wanting it: world3d imports ./live for the weather feature, live imports ./cloud, and cloud imports gl. A fixture compiles with no project manifest and CI installs nothing, so the package is simply absent there. The fix is to factor the pure geometry below (blockOf/foldIn/unfold/wrapPi and ringCount/ringOffset) out of the package-reaching import path — see docs/self-hosting.md; until then this is debt, stated out loud rather than dropped from the suite.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/flybyGeometry.milo`](../tests/fixtures/flybyGeometry.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-forInBlockScopeDrop

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `31`

**Rationale.** Block-scope drop must distinguish a for-in element (a borrow — not dropped by the loop; owned by the container) from a body-local owned value (dropped at the end of each iteration). Getting this wrong double-frees the Vec's strings. The sum is just a liveness check; the real assertion is "no double-free" (ASAN).

- `10`

- `20`

**Rationale.** Regression: `for x in makeVec()` over a temporary (rvalue) Vec silently ran zero iterations. genLValue returned "null" for the call, so ForEach GEP'd off a null base — reading a garbage len and never even emitting the producing call. ### MILO-B-forgetSuppressesDrop

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `done`

**Rationale.** `forget(x)` ends x's ownership WITHOUT running its drop — the one operation the move checker could not otherwise express, since every other way of consuming a value either drops it or hands it to something that will. It exists for the seams where ownership leaves through a raw pointer the checker cannot see (a closure environment memcpy'd into a scheduler task, a buffer handed to C), where the alternative is a double free or a leak dressed up as safety. Deliberately NOT on tests/leak-clean.txt: this program leaks 50 buffers on purpose, and that is the assertion. Fifty rather than one because a single forgotten pointer can still be sitting in a stack slot at exit, which conservative leak scanning reads as reachable — the one-value version of this test reported clean while leaking.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/forgetSuppressesDrop.milo`](../tests/fixtures/forgetSuppressesDrop.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-fstringEscapedBrace

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `{"id": "7"}`
- `{}`
- `a{b}c`

**Rationale.** An escaped brace in an f-string is a literal brace, not an interpolation opener. The lexer resolves `\{` to a sentinel so the parser's brace scan can tell the two apart; before that, everything up to the matching `}` was swallowed as an expression and the output was silently wrong.

### MILO-B-genericIterator

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `60`

**Rationale.** A user-defined generic iterator over a Vec-backed container, driven by `for x in`. Exercises three things at once: nested-generic inference (the `Vec<T>` field), the move of the Vec into the generic struct (no double-free), and the iterator protocol (next(&mut Self): Option<T>). A regression in the move handling turned the freed buffer's len into garbage and the loop ran away.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/genericIterator.milo`](../tests/fixtures/genericIterator.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-genericMethod

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `42`
- `v=21`
- `7`

**Rationale.** A method carrying its OWN type parameter — one not on the type it is implemented for. This did not work at all: impl methods are monomorphized eagerly when the STRUCT is instantiated, substituting only the struct's parameters, so a method-level `R` survived as a literal type name and `resolveMethod` handed back a signature mentioning it. The call reported `expected (&i64) => R, got (&i64) => i64` — naming a type nobody declared. Now the method is kept as a template and instantiated per call, the same lifecycle a generic free function gets. Two different `R` for one method are two functions, which is what the two calls below prove. Note both levels compose: `Box<T>` is already concrete in `T` (eagerly) and generic only in `R` (lazily) by the time the call site is reached. It also needed the structural unifier to descend into FUNCTION types, which it never did — that arm is why a callback can carry a type parameter at all.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/genericMethod.milo`](../tests/fixtures/genericMethod.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-genericMethodTrait

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `15`
- `n=5`

**Rationale.** A generic method declared in a TRAIT, not just an inherent impl. Inherent impls got method-level type parameters first, and the trait side was a hole in that work rather than a separate feature: `trait Mapper { fn apply<R>(…) }` did not even parse. Worth closing because a generic method is most useful as part of an interface a caller programs against, not only as a one-off on a concrete type. The template is keyed by receiver type and method name, with no trait in the key, because that is all a call site knows.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/genericMethodTrait.milo`](../tests/fixtures/genericMethodTrait.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-genericStructCycle

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `refused: owner held 7`
- `recovered 1.5`

**Rationale.** Regression: a generic struct reached again WHILE it is being instantiated. `Rejected<T>` holds an `Owner<T>`, and `Owner<T>`'s own method returns `Rejected<T>`, so resolving the field type walks back into the instantiation that started it. The memo for `Rejected_f64` used to be installed only after its fields resolved, so the re-entry re-ran the whole instantiation and registered every impl method a second time; LLVM then refused the program with `invalid redefinition of function`. It takes a second generic function over the pair to open the window, which is why the shape here is this specific. Nothing about it is shard-specific, so it gets a fixture of its own.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/genericStructCycle.milo`](../tests/fixtures/genericStructCycle.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-genericTraitDrop

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `made`
- `drop 5`
- `drop 9`

**Rationale.** A generic trait impl WITH a body — `impl Drop for Boxed<T>`. This used to fail with "unknown struct 'Boxed'": the body was checked eagerly against the base name (not a concrete struct). Generic trait impls now defer to per-monomorphization like inherent ones, so the body checks against Boxed_i64 and Drop is registered there. (Drops run in declaration order at scope exit — a and b give 5 then 9.)

### MILO-B-genericTypeAlias

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `9`
- `2`
- `1`
- `true`
- `10`
- `9`

**Rationale.** A type alias may take parameters, so a name can stand for a SHAPE rather than for one type: `Handler<T, R>` is the signature every callback in a module shares, written once. An alias is expanded at the use site, not instantiated: `Pair<i64>` IS `Vec<i64>` to everything downstream, so it needs no monomorphization, has no separate identity, and passes to anything expecting the underlying type. That is what makes it free, and it is also why a bound on an alias parameter is rejected: there would be no body to check it against.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/genericTypeAlias.milo`](../tests/fixtures/genericTypeAlias.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-getOrDefaultOwnedResult

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `Aabc`
- `Babc`
- `Cmiss`
- `Dvardefault`

**Rationale.** getOrDefault yields an OWNED value on both paths — a deep clone of the stored value when the key is present, the default itself when it is not. It sat in codegen's NOT_OWNED_TEMP list beside `contains` and `len`, which really do yield scalars, so a discarded or temporary result was never dropped and `print("A" + m.getOrDefault(k, d))` leaked the clone on every call. The list's own comment describes this exact miss for VecRemove: implicit is how a node inherits an answer nobody chose. Here the answer was chosen, and it was the wrong one. The printed values were always correct; tests/leak-clean.txt is what makes this a regression test. The miss path with a literal default never leaked (a literal owns no heap), which is most of why it survived.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/getOrDefaultOwnedResult.milo`](../tests/fixtures/getOrDefaultOwnedResult.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-globalBorrowArgSafe

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `byValue 1`
- `field 2`
- `whole 3`

**Rationale.** The three shapes the global-borrow-argument rule must NOT reject. Each passes something rooted at a mutable global to a function that writes that same global, and none of them can dangle: byValue — the parameter is by value, so the argument is materialised before the callee runs and nothing of G's is still borrowed. field — G.n names storage at G's own fixed address. A realloc inside G does not move it, and reassigning G overwrites it in place. whole — same, for G itself. Rejecting these is not hypothetical: it broke every entry point in milojs, whose interpreter state is a mutable global passed to functions that also write it. The reject case stays covered by tests/errors/globalBorrowArgRealloc.milo.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/globalBorrowArgSafe.milo`](../tests/fixtures/globalBorrowArgSafe.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-globalRuntimeInitScalar

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `1.25`
- `0.75`
- `6 7`
- `10`
- `14`
- `4`
- `true`
- `6`
- `24`

**Rationale.** A global with a runtime initializer is emitted zeroed and filled in by the generated init routine. The zero placeholder used to be the literal string "0", which is only valid LLVM for integer and i1 — so a scalar f64/f32 global, or a struct global with a non-constant float field, emitted `double 0` and clang rejected the whole module with an error[link] carrying no source location. Every global in globalRuntimeInit.milo is an aggregate, which already took the zeroinitializer branch; that is why nothing caught it. A closure-typed global additionally had its by-ref capture environment alloca'd in the init routine, so the env dangled the moment that frame went away.

### MILO-B-greenStackSize

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `deep recursion on a large green stack: ok`
- `default stack still works`

**Rationale.** Green tasks get a 1 MB stack, which is generous for ordinary work and far too small for a recursive interpreter — milojs budgets its JS call depth against the 8 MB the OS main thread provides. spawnWithStack lets a task ask for a comparable stack; the mapping is anonymous and lazily committed, so the larger reservation costs address space rather than resident memory.

### MILO-B-hashMapCloneDeep

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `true`
- `true`
- `true`
- `true`
- `true`
- `true`

**Rationale.** HashMap.clone() must deep-copy. A value copy of the {ptr,len,cap} header emits valid IR and passes any test that only reads the clone — the aliasing shows up only when one map is mutated and the other is read, or when both drop. This is a regression lock for the bug that broke self-hosting: the self-hosted compiler's checker does `ck.movedFields = snap.movedFields.clone()` to restore a move snapshot. With an aliasing clone, that restore was a no-op, so moves leaked out of one match arm into its sibling and the compiler rejected its own source.

### MILO-B-hashmapTombstoneReuse

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `maps=24`
- `wrong=0`

**Rationale.** A removed slot becomes a tombstone, and `insert` used to stop at the first one it met rather than probing on to see whether the key already lived further down the chain. That wrote a SECOND copy of a key already in the table: len over-counted, keys() listed it twice, and remove() deleted only one of the two. It needs a collision chain with a hole in it, so it depends on the hash seed. The seed is drawn per MAP, not per process, so building many maps in one run gives many independent seeds. One map reproduced the old bug about three runs in four; twenty-four of them makes a miss vanishingly unlikely, and the assertion is seed-independent: a map must end up holding exactly the keys the program put in it, once each.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/hashmapTombstoneReuse.milo`](../tests/fixtures/hashmapTombstoneReuse.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-heapPtrGive

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `same slot 1`
- `7 9`
- `adopted 7 9`
- `4 done`

**Rationale.** The give leg for a whole object: `h.ptr()` is the box pointer of a `Heap<T>`, the sibling of `v.ptr()` and the inverse of `adopt`. Before it existed, handing C a Milo-allocated struct meant `h.addrOf()` (the address of the SLOT holding the box pointer) and a load through it — an incantation, not an API. The first line proves the two are the same address, so the old spelling was right and this one is readable. The pointer is then given away for real: `forget` ends Milo's ownership and libc's `free` releases the allocation, which is the claim `Heap(v)` -> `malloc` makes. Running this under `scripts/asan-sweep.ts` is what checks it rather than asserts it.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/heapPtrGive.milo`](../tests/fixtures/heapPtrGive.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-heapPtrUserMethodWins

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `user ptr 42`

**Rationale.** `Heap<T>` resolves methods on T, so a user type with its own `ptr` method keeps it: the builtin give leg only answers when T has no such method. Without the guard, adding `Heap.ptr()` would have silently retargeted every existing `h.ptr()` call in the wild from the user's method to the box pointer.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/heapPtrUserMethodWins.milo`](../tests/fixtures/heapPtrUserMethodWins.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-hexStrict

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `rt=Hello`
- `empty=[]`
- `upper=foo`
- `badchar=hex: invalid digit at byte 0`
- `badsecond=hex: invalid digit at byte 3`
- `odd=hex: odd input length 3`
- `space=hex: odd input length 5`
- `prefix=hex: invalid digit at byte 1`

**Rationale.** Hex.decode is strict: even length, hex digits only. It used to map any non-digit to nibble 0 and drop a trailing odd digit, so "6 6f" and "zz" both decoded to bytes that looked like real data.

### MILO-B-httpFetchCoexist

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `200`
- `hi`
- `HTTP/1.1 200 OK`

**Rationale.** Windows is skipped for the same reason as fetchResponseParse: importing std/fetch links OpenSSL, which the xwin sysroot does not ship. One program that both serves HTTP and speaks the HTTP client. This did not compile until std/fetch's client response type was renamed off `Response`: the module namespace is flat and merged, so std/http's `Response` enum and std/fetch's struct were the same name, and importing both left one of them shadowed — "cannot access field on enum 'Response'" on a struct field read. Serving and fetching at once is an ordinary shape for a proxy or a webhook relay, so the collision blocked real programs.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/httpFetchCoexist.milo`](../tests/fixtures/httpFetchCoexist.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Type: text/plain; charset=utf-8\r\nContent-Length: 2\r\nConnection: close\r\nServer: milo\r\n\r\nhi`
- `HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found\r\nContent-Type: text/plain; charset=utf-8\r\nContent-Length: 13\r\nConnection: close\r\nServer: milo\r\n\r\n404 Not Found`
- `HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Type: application/json\r\nContent-Length: 2\r\nConnection: close\r\nServer: milo\r\n\r\n{}`
- `HTTP/1.1 201 Created\r\nContent-Type: text/html\r\nContent-Length: 3\r\nX-Trace: abc\r\nConnection: close\r\nServer: milo\r\n\r\n<b>`

**Rationale.** std/http's response wire format, pinned byte for byte. renderRaw/renderResponse were split out of sendRaw/sendResponse so std/https could write the same bytes through SSL_write. Nothing else asserts what those bytes are — the other http fixtures build a Context and never touch a socket — so a refactor of the builder could reorder or drop a header with every test still green.

### MILO-B-inflateCorruptInput

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `compressed-plausible=true`
- `all-outcomes-handled=true`
- `survived`

**Rationale.** std/inflate decodes bytes a program did not write — an upload, a response body, a file. Corrupt input must come back as `Err`, never a trap and never a read past a buffer. The module's twelve `requires` clauses are internal invariants and are dropped at -O2, so the property being pinned here is that the code VALIDATES before relying on them rather than leaning on the contracts (which is what std/pool and std/crypto were doing wrong). Mutating a VALID stream rather than feeding random bytes: 3000 random payloads behind a zlib header all failed at the header and never reached Huffman decoding at all. A single flipped byte in a real stream gets deep, and about 1% still decode.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/inflateCorruptInput.milo`](../tests/fixtures/inflateCorruptInput.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-ioSocketStream

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `server got: ping`
- `client got: pong`

**Rationale.** Not a Reader/Writer limitation: a std/net TcpListener.bind + TcpStream.connect round-trip with no streams in it at all dies the same way under Wine (no output, exit 9), which is why every other TcpStream fixture is skipped here too. The raw std/os socket path (greenThreadEcho) does run. Real Windows in CI is the authority; unskip when std/net itself is verified there. A line protocol over TCP through the Reader/Writer spine. The point of the borrowed `stream()` view: the same socket is read by a BufReader and written by a BufWriter at the same time, which an owning wrapper could not express.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/ioSocketStream.milo`](../tests/fixtures/ioSocketStream.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

The iteration contract itself — `next(&mut Self): Option<T>` — is checked where `for x in it` appears, so the trait needs no associated type.

### MILO-B-jsonAndThenChain

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `ada`
- `7`
- `missing`
- `-1`
- `missing`
- `ada`

**Rationale.** Chaining the owned Json accessors with Option.andThen. This is the replacement for the fixed-shape accessor family std/json used to carry (strAt/i64At/boolAt/getAt/childStrAt/…): every navigation shape needed its own method only because `get`/`at`/`path` returned an Option<Json> that could not be chained. One combinator covers all of them, at any depth. The zero-allocation spelling of the same walks is the cursor API (see jsonCursor.milo); each `get`/`at` hop deep-clones the subtree, which is the trade being made here.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/jsonAndThenChain.milo`](../tests/fixtures/jsonAndThenChain.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

**Rationale.** An integer read re-scans the literal's source span. The parser accumulates every number into an f64, so reading the stored f64 loses everything past 2^53 and silently truncates 1.5 to 1 — both are data loss with no signal.

### MILO-B-jsonSurrogatePairs

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `pair-bytes=4`
- `pair-equals-literal=true`
- `lone-high=3`
- `lone-low=3`
- `high-then-ascii=4`
- `bmp=2`

**Rationale.** JSON inherits JS's UTF-16 escapes, so a non-BMP character arrives as a SURROGATE PAIR: "\ud83d\ude00" is one code point, U+1F600. Emitting the halves separately gave two 3-byte sequences (CESU-8) — 6 bytes where UTF-8 needs 4, and not equal to the literal. Every surrogate is an invalid Unicode scalar value, so that was not valid UTF-8 at all, which is the one thing Milo's string type promises. Anything that stringifies with \u escaping (JS's JSON.stringify among them) sends these. Byte counts AND an equality check: a count alone would pass for any 4-byte answer. Lone surrogates have no UTF-8 encoding at all and become U+FFFD (3 bytes), which is what the rest of std/json already substitutes for a malformed sequence.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/jsonSurrogatePairs.milo`](../tests/fixtures/jsonSurrogatePairs.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

**Rationale.** Two shapes that read as work but are spelled-out versions of one std call. `arenaWith` exists to BORROW without copying; a closure whose whole body is `.clone()` throws the borrow away and is `arenaGet`. And a two-arm match over an Option that only supplies a default is `??`. Both are warnings, not errors: the code is correct, just longer than it needs to be. Compiled here so the fixture also proves the suggested rewrites agree.

### MILO-B-lookupKeyTempLeak

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `contains 30`
- `get-sum 110`
- `default-hit 110`
- `default-miss 110`
- `vec-found 30`
- `removed 30`
- `find-sum 160`

**Rationale.** A lookup takes the caller's key and only READS it. Nothing on that path takes ownership, so a key built at the call site — `m.get("k" + i.toString())`, the most ordinary map idiom there is — had no owner and leaked, one allocation per call. `insert` already knew this for the key it discards when overwriting; the six read-only lookups did not. getOrDefault leaked twice over: the key, and the DEFAULT whenever the key was found and the default went unused. This fixture exists to be leak-checked, not just to print the right numbers — the counts below were correct all along. tests/leak-clean.txt is what makes it a regression test.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/lookupKeyTempLeak.milo`](../tests/fixtures/lookupKeyTempLeak.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-matchBigAggSubject

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `drained 39`
- `empty`

**Rationale.** A match subject that is a temporary big aggregate (>= the 128-byte SSA threshold) is materialized into its alloca by memcpy, not by a whole-aggregate store. `replace` on a big enum hands back a POINTER to the moved-out value, so storing it as if it were the aggregate emitted invalid IR that only clang caught.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/matchBigAggSubject.milo`](../tests/fixtures/matchBigAggSubject.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-matchConsumesSubjectDrop

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `got 1`
- `drop 1`
- `after`

**Rationale.** A payload moved out of an enum by a pattern must leave the SUBJECT dead. Zeroing the payload is not enough on its own: it makes the subject's drop glue free a null pointer (a no-op, which is why heap payloads looked correct), but a user `Drop` impl runs regardless and receives a zeroed value. So `Res { id: 1 }` used to drop TWICE — once as the binding, once as the moved-from subject, printing `drop 0`. std/http's `Socket { fd: i32 }` is exactly this shape, and there the spurious second drop is `close(0)`: closing stdin on a socket already moved away. Only types whose fields are all Copy showed it, because a heap field's second free was silently a no-op.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/matchConsumesSubjectDrop.milo`](../tests/fixtures/matchConsumesSubjectDrop.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-moveClosureCaptureConsumed

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `52`
- `380`

**Rationale.** A `move` closure body that CONSUMES a capture, on both paths that release an environment: the closure value being dropped, and a detached OS thread finishing. Captures live in the environment's own slots, so moving one out zeroes the slot it came from — that zero is what tells the env drop glue the capture is already gone. When captures were copied into private allocas instead, the env kept a live header after the body had handed the value to a callee, so whichever path ran the glue freed it a second time. `Promise.blocking` sidestepped that by never running the glue at all, which leaked every captured handle (measured 120 leaks / 7360 bytes over 20 promises, now 0).

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/moveClosureCaptureConsumed.milo`](../tests/fixtures/moveClosureCaptureConsumed.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-moveClosureType

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `captured-heap`
- `non-owning closure`
- `bare fn value`
- `42`

**Rationale.** `move (T) => R` is the type of a closure that OWNS what it captured. It is a distinct type from a plain `(T) => R` for one reason: it is NOT Copy. Two copies of the pair would be two owners of one heap environment, which is why no destructor could exist for a closure before the split. A non-owning value (a by-reference closure, or a bare function) still passes to a `move` parameter — it owns nothing, so nothing is lost.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/moveClosureType.milo`](../tests/fixtures/moveClosureType.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

**Rationale.** The other half of per-field move tracking: a field that has been given a new value is live again, and moving a DIFFERENT field never made the first one unusable. Tracking moves per place is only correct if it also forgets them on assignment.

**Rationale.** Writing THROUGH a `&mut` is what one is for, so it counts as a use. ### MILO-B-optionAndThen

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `4`
- `-1`
- `-1`
- `3`
- `hi!`
- `true`

**Rationale.** Option.andThen(f): Option<T> -> Option<U>, where f itself returns an Option. The chaining form: without it a walk of fallible steps nests one Option per level and every level needs its own match. Three things this pins that map() does not: - the Some branch stores the callback's returned enum WHOLESALE (it is already an Option); re-tagging it as Some would wrap a None back into a Some. - a None ANYWHERE in the chain short-circuits, and the callback must not run. - the callback takes the payload by REF, so a non-Copy T is not moved out and the receiver stays usable afterwards. (Result.andThen consumes its receiver for a non-Copy E because it forwards the Err payload; None carries no payload, so the asymmetry is real, not an oversight.) Strings are built with pushStr, never written as literals: a literal has cap == 0, marking a static buffer that drop glue skips, which would hide a double-free.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/optionAndThen.milo`](../tests/fixtures/optionAndThen.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

- `true`
- `200`
- `5`

**Rationale.** Option.map(f): Option<T> -> Option<U>. The callback takes the payload by REF, which is what makes this safe without the Copy gate that unwrapOr/unwrapOrElse need: those load the payload out, so an owned inner would end up with two owners. map never moves it. Like unwrapOrElse, f must run ONLY on Some — the ordering of "mapped" below pins that.

### MILO-B-optionOrElse

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `fallback ran`
- `9`
- `1`
- `7`
- `true`
- `kept!`
- `fallback!`

**Rationale.** Option.orElse(f): Option<T> -> Option<T> — the None-side andThen. "Try this source, else that one", staying in Option so the chain can keep going. What this pins: - f runs ONLY on None; a Some receiver is forwarded verbatim and the fallback's own side effects (and allocations) never happen. - the Some branch forwards the receiver's payload into the result, so a non-Copy T CONSUMES the receiver — otherwise both would own one buffer and both free it. Asserted below by the fact that "kept!" comes back intact and exactly once. Strings are built with pushStr, never written as literals: a literal has cap == 0, marking a static buffer that drop glue skips, which would hide a double-free.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/optionOrElse.milo`](../tests/fixtures/optionOrElse.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-optionUnwrapOrElse

- `42`

**Rationale.** Option.unwrapOrElse(f) — unwrapOr with a lazily-computed default. The whole point is that f runs ONLY on None. unwrapOr lowers to a `select`, which evaluates both arms; reusing that here would call f even when Some, which is exactly what a caller picks unwrapOrElse to avoid. So this fixture asserts the side effect's absence via output ordering: "fallback ran" must appear once, after the 5.

### MILO-B-paramShadowsGlobal

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `2`
- `2`
- `99`

**Rationale.** A parameter must win over a global of the same name — innermost binding, as everywhere else in the language. Call resolution used to consult globals first, so a param could never shadow one. The real-world bite: a user defining `fn handler` broke std/http's *internal* `handler(ctx)` call, which resolved to the user's global instead of http's own parameter and reported `expected &mut Context, got Request` — a type error inside a file the user never opened. The workaround in the docs was to rename the user's function.

### MILO-B-poolBlockSizeGuard

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `too-small rejected`
- `zero-count rejected`
- `valid pool alloc ok`

**Rationale.** The free list threads an 8-byte pointer through each block, so a block SMALLER than 8 writes past the allocation. `requires size >= 8` said so, and a `requires` is dropped at the default -O2 — ASan reported a heap-buffer-overflow WRITE of size 8 for `Pool.new(2, 64)` in an ordinary build. Same shape as the AES-GCM read, one worse: a write. Sizes are derived at runtime here so the prover cannot reject them statically, which is what makes this a test of the RELEASE build rather than of the contract.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/poolBlockSizeGuard.milo`](../tests/fixtures/poolBlockSizeGuard.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-processPipeExecFail

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `launch failed`

**Rationale.** Launching a program that does not exist fails — but WHERE it fails differs by OS, and the fixture normalises both to the same observable outcome. On POSIX the exec can only fail post-fork inside the child, so spawn() succeeds and the failure shows up as a nonzero exit through wait(). On Windows CreateProcess resolves the program up front, so spawn() itself returns Err. Either way: the child never ran.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/processPipeExecFail.milo`](../tests/fixtures/processPipeExecFail.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-promiseCaptureDropOrder

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `worker done`
- `capture dropped`
- `await returned 7`

**Rationale.** A blocking Promise publishes its result over a channel, and the worker closure holds whatever it captured. If the capture is released only when the worker's frame is torn down, that happens AFTER the send, so a caller that looks at something the closure held - a Shared holder count, an open fd, a lock - can still see it alive after await() returned. sealShared asserted exactly such a count and went red on CI-linux for this reason while 280 local runs stayed green. The ordering below is the guarantee: every capture is gone before await() hands back a result. The 200ms in the destructor is what makes the assertion a GATE rather than a coin flip: with the release before the send, await() cannot return until the sleep is over, so the order holds on any platform; with the release left to frame teardown, the send has already happened and the main thread prints while the worker is still inside the destructor. Without the sleep the two orderings differ by microseconds and macOS happened to produce the right one either way, which would have made this fixture pass against the very bug it exists to catch.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/promiseCaptureDropOrder.milo`](../tests/fixtures/promiseCaptureDropOrder.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

**Rationale.** Regression: `?` in a function whose return type is a big aggregate (>128B, so it is sret-lowered to `void @f(ptr %__sret.out, …)`). The Err early-return must store into %__sret.out and `ret void`, not `ret <value>` — the latter mismatches the void result type and fails to compile (hades _decode). Must be a method: root free fns stay C-visible (by-value) and never sret, so only an internal fn (method or imported) exercises the broken path. Covers both propagate ret sites: same-enum (run) and From-conversion (runConv).

**Rationale.** A `*T` from `v.ptr()` / `s.cstr()` is an element view only while it is BOUND. These are the shapes the rule leaves alone: inline argument, pointer taken after the last mutation, pointer scoped to an inner block that ends before the mutation, a read of the source, and a move of the source (the buffer stays put; see tests/errors/ptrOutlivesMovedSource.milo).

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/ptrViewLegalShapes.milo`](../tests/fixtures/ptrViewLegalShapes.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-regexOnView

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `aab 1`
- `zzz 0`
- `aaab 1`
- `qqq 0`
- `true`
- `false`
- `true`
- `false`
- `3`
- `2..5`
- `7..9`
- `11..15`
- `1`
- `0`

**Rationale.** std/regex binds POSIX regcomp, which the MSVC CRT does not provide. A `&string` view is not NUL-terminated — it points into the middle of a bigger buffer. Before REG_STARTEND, regexec ran off the end of the view and reported matches from the REST of the buffer, silently. This locks that shut.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/regexOnView.milo`](../tests/fixtures/regexOnView.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-regexUtf8CodePoints

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `dot-accented=3`
- `dot-cjk=2`
- `class-ascii=2`
- `quantifier-multibyte=1`
- `zerowidth-accented=3`
- `zerowidth-cjk=2`
- `float=1.5`

**Rationale.** std/regex wraps POSIX regex, which asks the C LOCALE what a character is — and a program that never calls setlocale runs in the "C" locale, where a character is a BYTE. `.` therefore matched 4 times in "aéb": 3 code points, 4 bytes. Milo strings are UTF-8 by definition, so the matcher forces an LC_CTYPE that agrees. The float line is not decoration. The fix must not use LC_ALL: LC_NUMERIC decides the decimal separator, and moving it would rewrite every float the program prints. This pins that only the ctype category moved. The zero-width lines are a second bug the first one exposed. `findAll` advanced ONE BYTE after an empty match; once the locale was right, that landed inside a multibyte character, regexec rejected the start position, and the loop BROKE — silently returning a truncated list rather than a wrong one. It now advances a code point. Windows is skipped because that arm has no regex to fix — the MSVC CRT provides no C-linkable engine, so it aborts by design (std/regex.windows.milo).

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/regexUtf8CodePoints.milo`](../tests/fixtures/regexUtf8CodePoints.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-replaceFieldOutOfMutBorrow

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `hello`
- `[]`

**Rationale.** The escape hatch the move-out-of-a-borrow diagnostic points at for `&mut`. `return d.text` through a `&mut Doc` is rejected like any other move out of a borrow, but unlike the shared-`&` case there is a zero-copy answer: swap something in. `replace` moves the field out and leaves the replacement behind, so the owner is still whole and nothing is aliased or freed twice. The hint only offers this for `&mut` — through a shared `&` there is no write access and cloning is the only honest option.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/replaceFieldOutOfMutBorrow.milo`](../tests/fixtures/replaceFieldOutOfMutBorrow.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `5`
- `9`
- `2`
- `1`
- `30`

**Rationale.** replace(place, value) -> old and swap(a, b): move-in/move-out memory intrinsics that need no clone on T (they work on non-copyable types). Bodies can't be written in safe Milo, so they are compiler builtins; from the caller's view the moves are ordinary (a &mut borrow of the place plus a by-value move of value).

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/replaceSwap.milo`](../tests/fixtures/replaceSwap.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-resultAndThen

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `ok:4`
- `err:odd!`
- `err:upstream!`
- `ok:4`
- `err:odd!`
- `n=4!`

**Rationale.** Result.andThen(f): Result<T,E> -> Result<U,E>, where f itself returns a Result. Three things this pins that map() does not: - the Ok branch stores the callback's returned enum WHOLESALE (it is already a Result); re-tagging it as Ok would wrap an Err back into an Ok. - the Err branch forwards the receiver's error payload, so a short-circuited chain keeps the ORIGINAL error rather than a zeroed one. Asserted by value below. - that forwarded payload means a non-Copy E CONSUMES the receiver, otherwise receiver and result both own the error's buffer and both run drop glue on it. Errors below are built with pushStr, never written as literals: a literal has cap == 0, marking a static buffer that drop glue skips, which would hide the double-free. The callback's Err type must equal the receiver's — there is no conversion on the forwarding path (see tests/errors/resultAndThenErrMismatch.milo).

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/resultAndThen.milo`](../tests/fixtures/resultAndThen.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `ok:10`

- `err:42`
- `err:42`
- `ok:5`
- `ok:5`
- `ok:5`

**Rationale.** map/mapErr/andThen consume the receiver only when the payload they forward through is non-Copy. This pins the other half of that rule: with a Copy payload on the forwarded side, duplicating it is sound (no heap buffer, no drop glue), so the receiver must stay live and usable afterwards. Same Copy gate as unwrapOr. If the gate ever widens to consume unconditionally, this fixture stops compiling.

### MILO-B-resultMap

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `ok:42`
- `err:boom-alpha`
- `true`
- `mapped`
- `30`
- `0`
- `200`
- `12`
- `boom-carried`

**Rationale.** Result.map(f): Result<T,E> -> Result<U,E>. Two regressions this pins: - the Err branch has a payload to carry (unlike Option.map, whose None branch is empty). Store only the tag and the real error is silently replaced by a zeroed value — still an Err, so an isErr()-only assertion passes. Every Err case below asserts the error VALUE. - that forwarded Err payload leaves the receiver and the result both owning one buffer, so a non-Copy E must CONSUME the receiver. Payloads here are built with pushStr rather than written as literals on purpose: a literal has cap == 0, marking a static buffer that drop glue skips, which hides the double-free entirely. The callback takes the Ok payload by REF, so nothing is moved out of it — that is why there is no Copy gate on T.

### MILO-B-resultMapErr

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `err:4`
- `ok:99`
- `converting`
- `err:4`
- `ok:99`
- `val-hello`
- `code7`

**Rationale.** Result.mapErr(f): Result<T,E> -> Result<T,F> — the mirror image of map. Both regressions flip sides here: the OK branch is the one that forwards the source payload into the result. Miss the copy and `mapErr` on an Ok yields Ok(0) — still Ok, so an isOk()-only assertion would not catch it, hence every Ok case asserts the value. And because that Ok payload is forwarded, a non-Copy T must CONSUME the receiver. Ok payloads below are built with pushStr, never written as literals: a literal has cap == 0, marking a static buffer that drop glue skips, which would hide the double-free.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/resultMapErr.milo`](../tests/fixtures/resultMapErr.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-resultOrElse

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `ok:5`
- `err:-1`
- `ok:3`
- `kept!`
- `ok:8`

**Rationale.** Result.orElse(f): Result<T,E> -> Result<T,F> — the Err-side andThen. f receives the error and returns a whole Result, so a failed step can be recovered, retried, or have its error retyped without leaving Result. What this pins: - the Err branch stores the callback's returned enum WHOLESALE; re-tagging it as Err would turn a successful recovery back into a failure. - an Ok receiver short-circuits and forwards its payload untouched, so a non-Copy T CONSUMES the receiver (mirror of andThen, which forwards the Err payload). - F need not equal E — that is the whole difference from andThen, where the error type is pinned because it is the forwarded side. Strings are built with pushStr, never written as literals: a literal has cap == 0, marking a static buffer that drop glue skips, which would hide a double-free.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/resultOrElse.milo`](../tests/fixtures/resultOrElse.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-resultUnwrapOrElse

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `recovering`
- `5`
- `42`
- `3`
- `14`

**Rationale.** Result.unwrapOrElse(f) — unwrapOr with the default computed only on Err. It differs from Option.unwrapOrElse in the one place the two types genuinely differ: Result's failure carries information, so the callback RECEIVES THE ERROR (by ref, so nothing is moved out of the receiver). Option's failure carries nothing, so its callback takes no arguments. The other half of the point is laziness: unwrapOr lowers to a `select`, which evaluates both arms. Reusing that here would call f even on Ok — asserted below by output ordering, since "recovering" must appear only for the Err case. Errors are built with pushStr, never written as literals: a literal has cap == 0, marking a static buffer that drop glue skips, which would hide a double-free.

### MILO-B-saturatingMulSign

**Rationale.** Signed multiply overflows in whichever direction the true product points. codegen clamped to MAX either way, behind a comment calling it "a simplification — true saturation would check sign". It is not a simplification: (-2i8).saturatingMul(100) is -200, saturates to -128, and the old code answered 127 — wrong bound and wrong sign. saturatingAdd and saturatingSub always got this right, which is part of why it survived: the family looked tested.

### MILO-B-sealEqForeignSpan

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

**Rationale.** eq() used sealedHolds() as its only guard, and holds() answers "does this span fit here" — a foreign span makes it false, so eq() reported "not equal" for a question the caller never asked. Here b's bytes at 0..4 ARE "host", so the old code returned false while the true answer to the question asked was neither true nor false: the span was not b's to resolve. text() has always aborted on this; eq() now agrees with it.

### MILO-B-selectChildExit

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `pipe ok true`
- `arm 0`
- `reaped true`

**Rationale.** POSIX process model: fork/waitpid/kill have no Windows equivalent to bind to — CreateProcess is a different shape, not a translation. Child-exit Select arm — the leftover half of backlog C2. No new API was needed; the pieces just never existed at the same time: installSignalPipe(sigchld()) -> SIGCHLD makes an fd readable (the self-pipe trick) sel.onRead(fd) -> arm it like any other fd waitpid(pid, buf, WNOHANG) -> reap on wake without blocking the runtime SIGCHLD landed earlier today (it is the one signal whose number differs per platform: 20 darwin / 17 linux). WNOHANG is 1 on both (verified against sys/wait.h and linux/wait.h), so it needs no platform split. fd arms need a scheduler, so a green task must exist — see the limitation note at the top of std/select.milo. The timeout arm must NOT win: if it does, the signal fd never fired and the arm is broken.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/selectChildExit.milo`](../tests/fixtures/selectChildExit.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-selectFdVsChannel

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `fd woke: hi`

**Rationale.** the green scheduler's event loop is kqueue/epoll readiness-based; Windows needs the IOCP completion port rewrite (docs/roadmap.md). select over an fd read and a channel recv: a task writes the pipe, so the fd arm wins while the channel arm stays quiet — exercises the scheduler poll claiming a select and tearing down the still-armed channel arm

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/selectFdVsChannel.milo`](../tests/fixtures/selectFdVsChannel.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-selectMainContext

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `winner 0`
- `got 99`

**Rationale.** Select.wait() must return the WINNING ARM INDEX on the main context, not -1. It returned -1 for a long time: `schedulerCurrent()` is 0 on the main context, so `schedulerPark()` no-opped and the unclaimed -1 fell straight through to the caller. Select still woke at the right moment, so this looked like it worked — the demos just couldn't tell which arm fired and drained every arm to compensate. The fix is the shape channels already use (`_schedulerPollMain`): main can't park, because nobody else would drive the scheduler, so it polls a bounded tick and re-checks the claim. An earlier attempt that drove the poll from inside the wait lock hung. Arm 1 is a 5s timeout that must NOT win — if the poll loop ever stops making progress this fixture fails on the winner index rather than hanging the suite.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/selectMainContext.milo`](../tests/fixtures/selectMainContext.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-selectPromise

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `blocking arm 0`
- `blocking value 42`
- `green arm 0`
- `green value 7`

**Rationale.** A Promise can be armed in a Select via p.channel(). This is the composition that didn't exist: Promise.blocking runs its body on a foreign OS thread, Select waits on the green event loop, and there was no way to bridge one into the other — so an event-driven `timeout` had to poll instead. Promise has always held a Channel<T> internally; nothing exposed it. Both flavors are covered because they take different paths inside await(): `blocking` results arrive from a real pthread (the channel's cross-thread claim), `run` results from a green task.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/selectPromise.milo`](../tests/fixtures/selectPromise.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-selectTimerMain

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `timer arm 0`

**Rationale.** A timer-only Select on the main context must fire, not spin. Regression test for a hang I introduced fixing C1. The main-context wait loop polls via the scheduler and re-checks the claim — but `_schedulerRunOnce` returns EARLY when numTasks == 0, and even past that guard only polls the event loop while tasks remain. Select arms don't live on the task list (fd/timer arms hang off sSelFdHead and are claimed by _pollAndWake), so once the last green task finished, the poll became a no-op and the loop spun forever. Before C1 this returned -1 immediately — wrong, but it terminated. The spawned task is deliberately SHORT: it must be gone by the time the timer matters, which is exactly the condition that hangs. selectMainContext.milo doesn't catch this — its task is still alive when the claim lands.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/selectTimerMain.milo`](../tests/fixtures/selectTimerMain.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-shardMapWithClamp

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `2`
- `4`

**Rationale.** Regression: std/shard clamps the window count to one window per element, so asking for more windows than the buffer has elements produces fewer windows than were requested. parallelMapWith used to drive its collection loop off the requested count, so it waited on windows nobody would ever send and hung with no output. Over-partitioning a small input is what the function tells callers to do for load balance, so this is the recommended usage, not a corner.

### MILO-B-shardNoWorkers

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `refused: parallelMapWith: states is empty, so there is no worker to run on`
- `recovered undivided buffer: n=5 sum=15`
- `mapped after recovery: n=5 sum=30 seen=2`

**Rationale.** A refusal must not eat the buffer. This module's whole reason to exist is that a large Vec is never copied, so a refusal that dropped it would destroy the one thing the caller could not afford to lose over an empty Vec of states. The only refusal the closed forms can make is "no workers", and it carries the buffer home.

### MILO-B-shardParallelMap

- `n=16 first=3 last=3`
- `uneven n=10 sum=20`
- `one worker n=4 sum=8`

**Rationale.** parallelMap is the whole divide/run/reassemble cycle in one call, and it is infallible: this function creates every window, hands out every window, awaits all of them and welds them itself, so no caller code can drop one or let the owner die first. The completeness check inside the weld is an internal assertion, not an error a caller can see.

### MILO-B-signalSigchld

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `true`
- `true`

**Rationale.** POSIX process model: fork/waitpid/kill have no Windows equivalent to bind to — CreateProcess is a different shape, not a translation. SIGCHLD is the one signal in std/signal whose number differs per platform (20 darwin / 17 linux, verified against sys/signal.h and asm-generic/signal.h), so it comes from the std/platform split. Assert it against the value the OS itself reports rather than a hardcoded number — a literal here would just restate the bug it's meant to catch. The check: raise SIGCHLD at ourselves and confirm the handler receives the number we armed. If the constant were wrong for this platform, either the wrong signal fires (and the default action for it is likely to kill us) or nothing does. This also pins onSignal's contract — it took a `(i32) => void` closure, so the handler read the signal number out of the wrong register and saw garbage; a C signal handler must be a raw fn pointer because there is no slot for a captured env.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/signalSigchld.milo`](../tests/fixtures/signalSigchld.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-signalTwoPipes

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `winch->winch true`
- `winch->child false`
- `child->child true`
- `child->winch false`
- `rejected true`

**Rationale.** POSIX process model: fork/waitpid/kill have no Windows equivalent to bind to — CreateProcess is a different shape, not a translation. Two signals can each own a self-pipe at once. _sigPipeW used to be a single global i32, so installing a second signal silently re-pointed the shared handler at the second pipe. Raising SIGWINCH then made SIGCHLD's fd readable while the resize fd stayed empty — a resize delivered as a child exit. It survived because nothing had ever armed two signals at once; `timeout` arming SIGCHLD while `splitPty` arms SIGWINCH is what makes the pair reachable. Both directions are asserted: each signal must hit its OWN pipe and only its own.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/signalTwoPipes.milo`](../tests/fixtures/signalTwoPipes.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-sigpipeIgnored

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `write to closed pipe returned -1`
- `process survived`

**Rationale.** Windows has no SIGPIPE: a write to a broken pipe returns an error rather than raising a signal, so there is nothing for ignoreSigpipe() to guard against. The mechanism this fixture exercises does not exist on Windows. Writing to a pipe or socket whose reader has gone away raises SIGPIPE, and SIGPIPE's default action is to kill the process. A server that writes a response to a client that already hung up would die silently with status 141 — which is exactly what took down the milojs HTTP server under load. std/net ignores SIGPIPE when a listener or stream is created, so the write reports EPIPE and the program decides what to do.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/sigpipeIgnored.milo`](../tests/fixtures/sigpipeIgnored.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-sliceCombinators

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `7`
- `2`
- `4`
- `true`

**Rationale.** The read-only Vec combinators, on a slice. `&[T]` already carries the SAME `%Vec` layout a Vec does — `llvmType` returns `%Vec` for both — so these needed no new emitter. They were unavailable only because the checker and the lowerer each gate on `tag === "vec"`, which walled the whole combinator set off from every function taking a slice: `fn total(s: &[i64]) { return s.sum() }` reported *"type '[i64]' has no method 'sum'"*. The set is a WHITELIST, not a widening, because the same code paths carry `push`, `pop`, `insert` and `sort` — a slice is a non-owning view and must not grow or reorder the storage it points at. `s.push(1)` is still *"type '[i64]' has no method 'push'"*. The checker and the lowerer share one list (`SLICE_COMBINATORS` in src/types.ts) for a reason worth keeping: when only the checker was widened, the compiler ACCEPTED `s.sum()` and then died in lowering with `unsupported method call: sum` — a crash, not a diagnostic. Two gates that must agree should read from one place. Still NOT covered: a FIXED array (`[i64; 4]`). That is laid out inline as `[N x T]`, not as a `%Vec`, so it needs a real conversion rather than a re-tag — see backlog Tier 2 #6.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/sliceCombinators.milo`](../tests/fixtures/sliceCombinators.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

### MILO-B-sortByKeyString

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `Alice`

- `Carol`
- `Bob 25`

**Rationale.** A key extractor reading a string field must leave the container intact. The move-out-of-a-borrow rule exempts closure bodies, because the sort builtins read the extracted key and never drop it. The first version of that exemption fell through to the ordinary move path, which marks the field as moved — so codegen zeroed `u.name` *in the Vec being sorted* and every name came back empty, silently, with the sort still "working". No fixture covered a string key, so the whole suite stayed green.

### MILO-B-spawnCapturingClosureLeak

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `captured 42`
- `done`

**Rationale.** Every capturing spawn leaks its closure environment, and this fixture is NOT on tests/leak-clean.txt for that reason. It was, briefly: `reapTask` was made to free the env, which looked like ownership and was not — `fn` is a Copy type, so `Task.spawn(f)` gives the task a COPY of `{ptr fn, ptr env}` and the caller keeps an equally valid one. Freeing on the task's side turned a leak into a use-after-free (see spawnClosureStillValid.milo, which pins the safe behaviour). Fixing it for real means making an owning closure non-Copy so spawn MOVES it — docs/backlog.md #20 — and until then this program is a correctness test, not a leak test.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/spawnCapturingClosureLeak.milo`](../tests/fixtures/spawnCapturingClosureLeak.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

**Rationale.** Timer, Ticker and recvTimeout, on a main context that never spawned a green task of its own — the case std/select documents as inert, where a timer arm looks like protection and isn't. std/timer makes the scheduler exist first. Nothing here asserts an exact elapsed time: only ordering, "at least the interval", and generous ceilings that a loaded machine still clears.

- `same buffer: true`
- `h 104`
- `w 119`

**Rationale.** _strDataPtr must work on every shape a `&string` argument arrives in. A local and a struct field reach codegen as addresses; a `&string` PARAMETER arrives as the %String aggregate itself, passed by value, and GEPing that emitted `getelementptr %String, ptr <an SSA %String>` — invalid IR, so any function taking `s: &string` and asking for its data pointer failed to compile.

**Rationale.** Read-only windows over a string, divided and scanned by parallelScanStr. The interesting case is the seam: with disjoint windows a match straddling the boundary is found by neither side, which is why the call takes an overlap. Nothing writes, so the overlap is sound.

### MILO-B-stringCodePoints

A conforming implementation shall accept this program, and running it shall write exactly the lines below to standard output.

- `cp=97`
- `cp=233`
- `cp=28450`
- `cp=127881`
- `at 0 = 97`
- `at 1 = 233`
- `at 3 = 28450`
- `at 6 = 127881`
- `nonAscii=3`
- `first=97`
- `tmp cp=97`
- `tmp cp=233`
- `nested 97-120`
- `nested 97-121`
- `nested 98-120`
- `nested 98-121`
- `empty count=0`
- `s still usable len=10`

**Rationale.** `for b in s` yields BYTES (the right default for scanning ASCII delimiters); `for cp in s.codePoints()` yields decoded codepoints without materializing a Vec<i32>, so the UTF-8-correct loop costs no more to write than the shortcut. Desugars to a byte-cursor while loop, which is where the sharp edges are: the cursor advances BEFORE the body (or `continue` would spin forever), the index binding is the pre-advance offset, an rvalue receiver is bound once rather than re-evaluated per iteration, and nested loops need distinct cursors.

*Program:* [`tests/fixtures/stringCodePoints.milo`](../tests/fixtures/stringCodePoints.milo) — *verified by:* tests/run.test.ts — `fixtures (compile + run)`

Both are Option now, and the same parser backs std/strconv (see stdStrconv.milo). i64 range edges live in stringParseRange.milo — they can't round-trip through the JS backend's doubles.

**Rationale.** parseF64 hands the conversion to libc atof, which reads to a NUL. A `&string` view has no NUL at its own end, so an unbounded atof read "12345" out of a two-byte view of it. The parse must see only the view.
